\frac{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}^{\prime}(q)}{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q)}-\frac{1}{q(1-q)}=-\frac{D}{\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q)}
and the definition of
a_{Q}(q)
we find

\frac{a_{X}(x,y,q,\phi)}{x}=\left(a_{Q}(q)\left(\frac{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}^{\prime}(q)}{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q)}-\frac{1}{q(1-q)}\right)+\sigma\right)=0

In particular,
\hat{X}
has no quadratic variation term. Further, using
\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\hat{\Phi}_{t}}{\hat{\Phi}_{t}}=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}G^{\uparrow}_{t}}{\hat{Q}_{t}}-\frac{\,\mathrm{d}G^{\downarrow}_{t}}{\hat{Q}_{t}}=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}G}{\hat{Q}_{t}}
and the fact that
\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}^{\prime}=0
at
q_{*}
and
q^{*}
,

\frac{c_{X}(x,y,q,\phi)}{x}=\frac{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}^{\prime}(q)}{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q)}-\frac{1}{q(1-q)}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{-1}{1-q}.

Then
c_{t}\,\mathrm{d}G_{t}=-\frac{\hat{X}_{t}}{1-\hat{Q}_{t}}\,\mathrm{d}G_{t}=-\frac{\hat{X}_{t}\hat{Q}_{t}}{1-\hat{Q}_{t}}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\hat{\Phi}_{t}}{\hat{\Phi}_{t}}=-Y_{t}\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(\hat{Q}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}\hat{\Phi}_{t}
.
Finally,

\displaystyle\frac{b_{X}(x,y,q,\phi)}{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}^{\prime}(q)}{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q)}-\frac{1}{(1-q)}-\frac{1}{q}\right)b_{Q}(q)+\mu-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\frac{a_{Q}(q)^{2}}{2}\left(-\frac{1}{(1-q)^{2}}+\frac{1}{q^{2}}+\frac{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}^{\prime\prime}(q)}{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q)}-\left(\frac{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}^{\prime}(q)}{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q)}\right)^{2}\right).

Then, differentiating
(1-q)q\frac{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}^{\prime}(q)}{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q)}=\frac{m(q)-n_{\xi}(q)}{\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q)}
and using
\ell(q)=m(q)+Dq(1-q)
,
\ell^{\prime}(q)-m^{\prime}(q)=D(1-2q)
and
\ell(q)m^{\prime}(q)-m(q)\ell^{\prime}(q)=D(q(1-q)m^{\prime}(q)-(1-2q)m)
yields

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}^{\prime\prime}(q)}{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q)}-\left(\frac{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}^{\prime}(q)}{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{(1-2q)}{q^{2}(1-q)^{2}}\frac{m(q)-n_{\xi}(q)}{\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q)}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{(\ell(q)m^{\prime}(q)-m(q)\ell^{\prime}(q))+n_{\xi}(q)(\ell^{\prime}(q)-m^{\prime}(q))-n^{\prime}(q)(\ell(q)-m(q))}{q(1-q)(\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q))^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{(1-2q)}{q^{2}(1-q)^{2}}\frac{(m(q)-n_{\xi}(q))}{(\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q))}

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84526pt+\frac{D}{q(1-q)(\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q))^{2}}\left(q(1-q)m^{\prime}(q)-(1-2q)(m(q)-n_{\xi}(q))-q\frac{1-S}{S}n_{\xi}(q)\frac{m(q)-n_{\xi}(q)}{\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q)}\right)

It follows that, using
m^{\prime}(q)=\frac{R(1-S)}{S}\sigma^{2}q-\frac{(1-S)}{S}(\mu-r)
,

\displaystyle\frac{b_{X}(x,y,q,\phi)}{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{Dq}{\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q)}\left(n_{\xi}(q)-2\theta S(\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q))\right)+\mu-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}(\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q))^{2}}{2D^{2}}\frac{(1-q)^{2}-q^{2}}{(1-q)^{2}q^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}(\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q))^{2}\frac{(1-2q)(m(q)-n_{\xi}(q))}{(\ell(q)-m(q))^{2}(\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q))}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2(\ell(q)-m(q))}\left(q(1-q)\left(\frac{R(1-S)}{S}\sigma^{2}q-\frac{(1-S)}{S}(\mu-r)\right)-(1-2q)(m(q)-n_{\xi}(q))\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\left.-q\frac{1-S}{S}\frac{n_{\xi}(q)(m(q)-n_{\xi}(q))}{\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}+q\sigma^{2}(1-R)-\frac{Dqn_{\xi}(q)}{(\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q))}+\left(R\sigma^{2}q-(\mu-r)\right)+(1-2q)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\frac{(\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q))^{2}}{(\ell(q)-m(q))^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-(1-2q)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\frac{(\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q))(m(q)-n_{\xi}(q))}{(\ell(q)-m(q))^{2}}-(1-2q)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\frac{(m(q)-n_{\xi}(q))}{(\ell(q)-m(q))}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-q\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\frac{(1-S)}{S}\frac{n_{\xi}(q)(m(q)-n_{\xi}(q)}{(\ell(q)-m(q))(\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r-\frac{Dqn_{\xi}(q)}{\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q)}\left(1+\frac{m(q)-n_{\xi}(q)}{\ell(q)-m(q)}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+(1-2q)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\left[-1+\frac{(\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q))^{2}}{(\ell(q)-m(q))^{2}}-\frac{(\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q))(m(q)-n_{\xi}(q))}{(\ell(q)-m(q))^{2}}-\frac{(m(q)-n_{\xi}(q))}{(\ell(q)-m(q))}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r-\frac{n_{\xi}(q)}{1-q}

Then
b_{t}=r\hat{X}_{t}-\frac{\hat{X}_{t}}{1-\hat{Q}_{t}}n(\hat{Q}_{t})=r\hat{X}_{t}-\frac{Y_{t}\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(\hat{Q}_{t})\hat{\Phi}_{t}}{\hat{Q}_{t}}n(\hat{Q}_{t})=r\hat{X}_{t}-\hat{C}_{t}
.
Combining the expressions for
a_{t}
,
b_{t}
and
c_{t}
we conclude that
\hat{X}
does indeed solve (
A.64
).

It only remains to show that
(\hat{\Phi},\hat{C})\in\mathscr{A}
. We consider the case where
q_{*},q^{*}>0
. Then
\Phi_{t}>0
and it is sufficient to show that
\hat{X}_{t}+\frac{\hat{\Phi}_{t}Y_{t}}{\gamma^{\downarrow}}>0
. Substituting for
\hat{X}_{t}
using (
5.33
) we have
\hat{X}_{t}+\frac{\hat{\Phi}_{t}Y_{t}}{\gamma^{\downarrow}}=\hat{\Phi}_{t}Y_{t}\left(\frac{1-\hat{Q}_{t}}{\hat{Q}_{t}}\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(\hat{Q}_{t})+\frac{1}{\gamma^{\downarrow}}\right)
. Set
\chi(q)=\frac{(1-q)}{q}\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q)+\frac{1}{\gamma^{\downarrow}}
: we want to show that
\chi(q)>0
on
(q_{*},q^{*})
. The result is immediate on
(q_{*}\wedge 1,1]
so it is sufficient to consider
\chi
on
[q_{*}\vee 1,q^{*})
. But on this interval
\chi^{\prime}(q)=-\frac{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q)}{q^{2}}\left(\frac{\ell(q)-m(q)}{\ell(q)-n_{\xi}(q)}\right)<0
and the positivity of
\chi
on
[q_{*}\vee 1,q^{*})
follows from the monotonicity of
\chi
and the fact that
\chi(q^{*})=\frac{1}{q^{*}\gamma^{\downarrow}}
.
∎

Proposition A.7
.

Let
\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}\in[1,\infty)
with
\xi:=\gamma^{\uparrow}\gamma^{\downarrow}>1
be such that
\xi
is in the well-posedness range of transaction costs. Let
(x,y,\phi)\in\mathscr{S}^{\circ}_{(\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow})}
.
Let
(\hat{\Phi},\hat{C})\in\mathscr{A}_{(\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow})}(x,y,\phi)
be the candidate optimal investment-consumption pair,
\hat{X}=(\hat{X}_{t})_{t\geq 0}:=(X^{x,y,\phi,\hat{\Phi},\hat{C}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
the corresponding cash process, and
\hat{Q}=(\hat{Q}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
the corresponding shadow fraction of wealth.
Then

\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(t,\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\hat{\Phi}_{t})=\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(0,\hat{X}_{0},Y_{0},\hat{\Phi}_{0}){\mathcal{E}}(\sigma(1-R)\hat{Q}\bullet B)_{t}e^{-\theta\int_{0}^{t}n_{\xi}(\hat{Q}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s},\hskip 10.00002ptt\geq 0.

(A.65)

Moreover, the process
\hat{M}=(\hat{M}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
defined by

\hat{M}_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}g_{EZ}(s,\hat{C}_{s},\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(s,\hat{X}_{s},Y_{s},\hat{\Phi}_{s}))\,\mathrm{d}s+\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(t,\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\hat{\Phi}_{t}).

is a uniformly integrable martingale with
\hat{M}_{\infty}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}g_{EZ}(s,\hat{C}_{s},\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(s,\hat{X}_{s},Y_{s},\hat{\Phi}_{s}))\,\mathrm{d}s

Proof.

First, it follows from the definition of
\hat{C}
in Theorem
5.13
(c), (
A.49
) and (
A.52
) that

g_{EZ}(t,\hat{C}_{t},\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(t,\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\hat{\Phi}_{t}))=\theta n_{\xi}(\hat{Q}_{t})\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(t,\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\hat{\Phi}_{t}),\hskip 10.00002ptt\geq 0.

(A.66)

Second, Itô’s formula, Proposition
A.5
(a) and (b) and the properties of
\hat{\Phi}
,
\hat{C}
from Theorem
5.13
give

\,\mathrm{d}\hat{M}_{t}=\sigma(1-R)\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(t,\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\hat{\Phi}_{t})\hat{Q}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t},

(A.67)

Third, combining (
A.67
) with (
A.66
) yields

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{d}\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(t,\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\hat{\Phi}_{t})

\displaystyle=\,\mathrm{d}\hat{M}_{t}-g_{EZ}(t,\hat{C}_{t},\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(t,\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\hat{\Phi}_{t}))\,\mathrm{d}t

(A.68)

\displaystyle=\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(t,\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\hat{\Phi}_{t})\left(\sigma(1-R)\hat{Q}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}-\theta n_{\xi}(\hat{Q}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\right).

(A.69)

The formula for the stochastic exponential now gives (
A.65
).

Next, fix
\beta>0
and let
C>0
be a uniform bound for
\hat{Q}
and
\nu>0
a lower bound for
n_{\xi}
. Since
\hat{Q}
is uniformly bounded, Novikov’s condition implies that
{\mathcal{E}}(\beta\sigma(1-R)\hat{Q}\bullet B)
is a true martingale. Hence, (
A.65
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(t,\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\hat{\Phi}_{t})|^{\beta}\right]

\displaystyle=|\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(0,\hat{X}_{0},Y_{0},\hat{\Phi}_{0})|^{\beta}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\beta(\beta-1)\frac{\sigma^{2}(1-R)^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\hat{Q}_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s-\theta\int_{0}^{T}n_{\xi}(\hat{Q}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s}\right]

(A.70)

\displaystyle\leq|\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(0,\hat{X}_{0},Y_{0},\hat{\Phi}_{0})|^{\beta}e^{\beta(\beta-1)\frac{\sigma^{2}(1-R)^{2}}{2}C^{2}-\theta\nu)t},

(A.71)

where
C
is a (upper or lower as required) bound on
\hat{Q}
, and
\nu
is a lower bound on
n_{\xi}
.
For
\beta=2
, (
A.71
) shows that
\hat{M}
is a square-integrable martingale. For
\beta=1
, (
A.71
) shows that
\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[|\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(t,\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\hat{\Phi}_{t})|\right]=0
and together with (
A.66
) and the fact that
n_{\xi}
is uniformly bounded, this implies that
\hat{M}_{t}
converges in
L^{1}
to
\hat{M}_{\infty}:=\int_{0}^{t}g_{EZ}(s,\hat{C}_{s},\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(s,\hat{X}_{s},Y_{s},\hat{\Phi}_{s}))\,\mathrm{d}s
. Hence,
\hat{M}
is a uniformly integrable martingale.
∎

Definition A.8
.

Suppose that
X=(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
Y=(Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
are nonnegative progressive processes. We say that
X
has the same order as
Y
(and write
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathds{O}}}{{=}}Y
, noting that
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathds{O}}}{{=}}
is an equivalence relation) if there exist constants
k,K\in(0,\infty)
such that

0\leq kY\leq X\leq KY.

(A.72)

Denote the set of progressive processes with the same order as
X
by
\mathds{O}(X)\subseteq\mathscr{P}_{+}
.

Definition A.9
.

For
X\in\mathscr{P}_{+}
, define
J^{X}=(J^{X}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
by
J^{X}_{t}=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{\infty}X_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]
. Then the set
\mathds{S}\mathds{O}
of
self-order
consumption streams is given by

\mathds{S}\mathds{O}\coloneqq\left\{X\in\mathscr{P}_{++}:\penalty\ \mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}X_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]<\infty\mbox{ and }X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathds{O}}}{{=}}J^{X}\right\}.

(A.73)

Moreover, set

\mathds{S}\mathds{O}_{\nu}\coloneqq\left\{X\in\mathds{S}\mathds{O}:\penalty\ \big(e^{\nu t}X_{t}\big)_{t\geq 0}\in\mathds{S}\mathds{O}\right\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }\nu\geq 0,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\mathds{S}\mathds{O}_{+}=\cup_{\nu>0}\mathds{S}\mathds{O}_{\nu}.

Lemma A.10
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathscr{P}_{+}
with
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathds{O}}}{{=}}Y
. Then for
\nu\geq 0
,
X\in\mathds{S}\mathds{O}_{\nu}
if and only if
Y\in\mathds{S}\mathds{O}_{\nu}
. In particular,
X\in\mathds{S}\mathds{O}_{+}
if and only if
Y\in\mathds{S}\mathds{O}_{+}
.

Corollary A.11
.

Let
\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}\in[1,\infty)
with
\xi:=\gamma^{\uparrow}\gamma^{\downarrow}>1
be such that
\xi
lies in the well-posedness range of transaction costs. Let
(x,y,\phi)\in\mathscr{S}^{\circ}_{(\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow})}
.
Let
(\hat{\Phi},\hat{C})\in\mathscr{A}_{(\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow})}(x,y,\phi)
be the candidate optimal investment-consumption pair,
\hat{X}=(\hat{X}_{t})_{t\geq 0}:=(X^{x,y,\phi,\hat{\Phi},\hat{C}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
the corresponding cash process, and
\hat{Q}=(\hat{Q}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
the corresponding shadow fraction of wealth. Then the process
(e^{-\delta\theta t}\hat{C}^{1-R}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
lies in
\mathds{S}\mathds{O}_{+}
.

Further,
\hat{C}
is uniquely proper.

Proof.

First, note that

e^{-\delta\theta t}\hat{C}^{1-R}_{t}=e^{-\delta\theta t}(\hat{X}_{t}+\hat{\Phi}_{t}\tilde{Y}_{t})^{1-R}n_{\xi}(\hat{Q}_{t})^{1-R}=(1-R)\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(t,\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\hat{\Phi}_{t})n_{\xi}(\hat{Q}_{t})^{\theta}

Since
n_{\xi}
is uniformly bounded from above and from below, by Lemma
A.10
it remains to show that
that
((1-R)\hat{V}(t,\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\hat{\Phi}_{t}))_{t\geq 0}\in\mathds{S}\mathds{O}_{+}
.

To this end, it suffices to show that
((1-R)\hat{V}(t,\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\hat{\Phi}_{t}))_{t\geq 0}\in\mathds{S}\mathds{O}_{\nu}
for some
\nu>0
. Since
n
is uniformly bounded from above and below by a positive constants, there is
D>\nu>0
such that
D\geq\theta n+\nu\geq\nu>0
. Now using that
{\mathcal{E}}(\sigma(1-R)\hat{Q}\bullet B)_{t}
is a martingale by Novikov’s condition and the fact that
\hat{Q}
is uniformly bounded, we obtain

\displaystyle J_{t}^{\exp(\nu\cdot)\hat{V}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{\infty}\exp(\nu s)(1-R)\hat{V}(s,\hat{X}_{s},Y_{s},\hat{\Phi}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=(1-R)\hat{V}(t,\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\hat{\Phi}_{t})\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\int_{t}^{s}\theta n(\hat{Q}_{u})-\nu\,\mathrm{d}u\right)\,\mathrm{d}s\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]

(A.74)

The claim that
(e^{-\delta\theta t}\hat{C}^{1-R}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
lies in
\mathds{S}\mathds{O}_{+}
now follows from the fact that the last factor on the right hand side of (
A.74
) lies in
[\frac{1}{D},\frac{1}{\nu}]
.

The fact that there is a unique utility process
V^{\hat{C}}
associated to
\hat{C}
now follows from Herdegen et al
[
13
, Theorem 4.7]
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.15
.

First, it follows from Proposition
A.11
and Theorem
5.13
that
\hat{C}
is uniquely proper and has continuous paths.

Next, define the process
\hat{M}=(\hat{M}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
by

\hat{M}_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}g_{EZ}(s,\hat{C}_{s},\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(s,\hat{X}_{s},Y_{s},\hat{\Phi}_{s}))\,\mathrm{d}s+\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(t,\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\hat{\Phi}_{t}).

By Proposition
A.7
,
\hat{M}
is a uniformly integrable martingale with

\hat{M}_{\infty}=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\delta s}\frac{(\hat{C}_{s})^{1-S}}{1-S}((1-R)\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(s,\hat{X}_{s},Y_{s},\hat{\Phi}_{s}))^{\rho}\,\mathrm{d}s.

This implies that

\displaystyle\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(t,\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\hat{\Phi}_{t})

\displaystyle=\hat{M}_{t}-\int_{0}^{t}g_{EZ}(s,\hat{C}_{s},\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(s,\hat{X}_{s},Y_{s},\hat{\Phi}_{s}))\,\mathrm{d}s

(A.75)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{M}_{\infty}\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]-\int_{0}^{t}g_{EZ}(s,\hat{C}_{s},\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(s,\hat{X}_{s},Y_{s},\hat{\Phi}_{s}))\,\mathrm{d}s

(A.76)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{\infty}g_{EZ}(s,\hat{C}_{s},\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(s,\hat{X}_{s},Y_{s},\hat{\Phi}_{s}))\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{t}\right].

(A.77)

Thus
(\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(t,\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\hat{\Phi}_{t})_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a utility process for
\hat{C}
. Note that it is never vanishes and hence is a proper solution. Thus, it coincides with
V^{\hat{C}}
by uniqueness of utility processes (for proper solutions).
∎

A.4
Verification argument

Proof of Proposition
5.17
.

First, note that the dynamics of
X+\varepsilon\hat{X}
are given by

\mathrm{d}(X_{t}+\varepsilon\hat{X}_{t})=r(X_{t}+\varepsilon\hat{X}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t-(C_{t}+\varepsilon\hat{C}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t-Y_{t}\gamma^{\uparrow}\,\mathrm{d}(\Phi_{t}^{\uparrow}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}^{\uparrow}_{t})+\frac{Y_{t}}{\gamma^{\downarrow}}\,\mathrm{d}(\Phi_{t}^{\downarrow}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{t}^{\downarrow}).

(A.78)

Next, fix arbitrary bounded stopping times
\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
. Using Itô’s formula, Proposition
A.5
, Corollary
A.6
and the fact that
\Phi_{t}^{\uparrow}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{t}^{\uparrow}-(\Phi_{t}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{t})^{\uparrow}
and
\Phi_{t}^{\downarrow}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{t}^{\downarrow}-(\Phi_{t}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{t})^{\downarrow}
are nondecreasing and identical for
t\geq 0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{V}(X_{\tau_{2}}+\varepsilon\hat{X}_{\tau_{2}},Y_{\tau_{2}},\Phi_{\tau_{2}}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{\tau_{2}})-\hat{V}(X_{\tau_{1}}+\varepsilon\hat{X}_{\tau_{1}},Y_{\tau_{1}},\Phi_{\tau_{1}}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{\tau_{1}})

(A.79)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}g_{EZ}(C_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{C}_{u},\hat{V}(X_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{X}_{u},Y_{u},\Phi_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{u}))\,\mathrm{d}u

(A.80)

\displaystyle=\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\left(\hat{V}_{u}\,\mathrm{d}u+\hat{V}_{x}\,\mathrm{d}(X_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{X}_{u})+\hat{V}_{y}\,\mathrm{d}Y_{u}+\hat{V}_{\phi}\,\mathrm{d}(\Phi_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{u})+\frac{1}{2}\hat{V}_{yy}\,\mathrm{d}\langle Y\rangle_{u}+g_{EZ}(C_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{C}_{u},\hat{V})\,\mathrm{d}u\right)

(A.81)

\displaystyle=\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\left(\hat{V}_{t}+r(X_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{X}_{u})\hat{V}_{x}-(C_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{C}_{u})\hat{V}_{x}+\mu Y_{u}\hat{V}_{y}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}Y_{u}^{2}\hat{V}_{yy}+g_{EZ}(C_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{C}_{u},\hat{V})\right)\,\mathrm{d}u

(A.82)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\left(\hat{V}_{\phi}-\gamma^{\uparrow}Y_{u}\hat{V}_{x}\right)\,\mathrm{d}(\Phi_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{u})^{\uparrow}-\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\gamma^{\uparrow}Y_{u}\hat{V}_{x}\,\mathrm{d}\left(\Phi_{u}^{\uparrow}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{u}^{\uparrow}-(\Phi_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{u})^{\uparrow}\right)

(A.83)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\left(\frac{Y_{u}}{\gamma^{\downarrow}}\hat{V}_{x}-\hat{V}_{\phi}\right)\,\mathrm{d}(\Phi_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{u})^{\downarrow}+\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\frac{Y_{u}}{\gamma^{\downarrow}}\hat{V}_{x}\,\mathrm{d}\left(\Phi_{u}^{\downarrow}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{u}^{\downarrow}-(\Phi_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{u})^{\downarrow}\right)

(A.84)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\sigma Y_{u}\hat{V}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{u}

(A.85)

\displaystyle\leq-\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\hat{V}_{x}Y_{u}\left(\gamma^{\uparrow}-\frac{1}{\gamma^{\downarrow}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\left(\Phi_{u}^{\uparrow}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{u}^{\uparrow}-(\Phi_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{u})^{\uparrow}\right)+\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\sigma Y_{u}\hat{V}_{y}\,\mathrm{d}W_{u}\leq\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}\sigma Y_{u}\hat{V}_{y}\,\mathrm{d}W_{u}.

(A.86)

Define the local martingale
N=(N_{t})_{t\geq 0}
by

N_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma Y_{u}\hat{V}_{y}(u,X_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{X}_{u},Y_{u},\Phi_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{u})\,\mathrm{d}W_{u}.

(A.87)

Moreover, for
n\in\mathds{N}
, define the stopping time
\zeta_{n}\coloneqq\inf\{s\geq\tau_{1}:\langle{N}\rangle_{s}-\langle{N}\rangle_{\tau_{1}}\geq n\}
. Then

\displaystyle\hat{V}(X_{\tau_{1}}+\varepsilon\hat{X}_{\tau_{1}},Y_{\tau_{1}},\Phi_{\tau_{1}}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{\tau_{1}})\geq\hat{V}(X_{\tau_{2}\wedge\zeta_{n}}+\varepsilon\hat{X}_{\tau_{2}\wedge\zeta_{n}},Y_{\tau_{2}\wedge\zeta_{n}},\Phi_{\tau_{2}\wedge\zeta_{n}}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{\tau_{2}\wedge\zeta_{n}})

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}\wedge\zeta_{n}}e^{-\delta u}\frac{(C_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{C}_{u})^{1-S}}{1-S}((1-R)\hat{V}(u,X_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{X}_{u},S_{u},\Phi_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{u}))^{\rho}\,\mathrm{d}u+N_{\tau_{1}}-N_{{\tau_{2}\wedge\zeta_{n}}}.

Taking conditional expectations and using that
(N_{t\wedge\zeta_{n}}-N_{t\wedge\tau_{1}})_{t\geq 0}
is an
L^{2}
-bounded martingale, the Optional Sampling Theorem gives

\hat{V}_{\tau_{1}}\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{V}_{\tau_{2}\wedge\zeta_{n}}+\int_{\tau_{1}}^{{\tau_{2}}\wedge\zeta_{n}}e^{-\delta u}\frac{(C_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{C}_{u})^{1-S}}{1-S}((1-R)\hat{V}_{u})^{\rho}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{1}}\right].

(A.88)

where in a slight abuse of notation we have written
\hat{V}_{u}
as shorthand for
\hat{V}(u,X_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{X}_{u},Y_{u},\Phi_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{u})
.
Since
\hat{V}_{u}\geq\hat{V}(u,\varepsilon\hat{X}_{u},Y_{u},\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{u})
by Corollary
A.6
and using
(\hat{V}(t,\varepsilon\hat{X}_{t},Y_{t},\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{t}))_{t\geq 0}
is bounded below by zero if
R<1
and by a uniformly integrable martingale if
R>1
by Proposition
A.7
, taking the liminf as
n\to\infty
in (
A.88
), the conditional version of Fatou’s Lemma (with a UI martingale lower bound if
R>1
) and the conditional Monotone Convergence Theorem yield

\hat{V}_{\tau_{1}}\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{V}_{\tau_{2}}+\int_{\tau_{1}}^{\tau_{2}}e^{-\delta u}\frac{(C_{u}+\varepsilon\hat{C}_{u})^{1-S}}{1-S}((1-R)\hat{V}_{u})^{\rho}\,\mathrm{d}u\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{1}}\right].

(A.89)

Furthermore,
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\hat{V}_{t}]\geq\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\hat{V}(t,\varepsilon\hat{X}_{t},S_{t},\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{t}))]=0
. Consequently,
\hat{V}(I,X+\varepsilon\hat{X},Y,\Phi+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi})
is a supersolution for
C+\varepsilon\hat{C}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.18
.

The argument is different for
\theta\leq 1
and
\theta>1
.

Case
\theta\leq 1
.

By Theorem
5.15
, Proposition
A.5
(c) and continuity of
\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}
, it suffices to show that for each
\varepsilon>0
and
C\in\mathscr{C}_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(x,y,\phi)
,

V^{C}_{0}\leq\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(0,x(1+\varepsilon),y,\phi(1+\varepsilon)).

(A.90)

So fix
\varepsilon>0
and
C\in\mathscr{C}^{(\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow})}(x,y,\phi)
with corresponding investment process
\Phi
and cash process
X=X^{x,y,\phi,\Phi,C}
. Moreover, set
\hat{X}=\hat{X}^{x,y,\phi,\hat{\Phi},\hat{C}}

The cases
R<1
and
R>1
require slightly different arguments.

First, assume
R<1
. Then
\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(I,X+\varepsilon\hat{X},Y,\Phi+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi})
is a supersolution for the pair
(g_{EZ},C+\varepsilon\hat{C})
by Proposition
5.17
. Since
C+\varepsilon\hat{C}>C
and
\frac{c^{1-S}}{1-S}
is increasing in
c
,
\hat{V}_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(I,X+\varepsilon\hat{X},Y,\Phi+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi})
is also supersolution for
C
by (
A.89
). Moreover, the (generalised) utility process
V^{C}
for
C
is the minimal supersolution for
C
by
[
12
, Theorem 6.5]
. Consequently,
\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(I,X+\varepsilon\hat{X},Y,\Phi+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi})\geq V^{C}
, and (
A.90
) follows from considering
t=0
and using Corollary
A.6
(b).

Next, assume
R>1
, which implies
S>1
by the fact that
\theta>0
. Since
(C+\varepsilon\hat{C})^{1-S}\leq(\varepsilon\hat{C})^{1-S}
,
C+\varepsilon\hat{C}
is uniquely evaluable by Corollary
A.11
and
[
12
, Corollary 6.3]
. Hence, there exists a uniformly integrable utility process
V^{C+\varepsilon\hat{C}}
, which is in particular a subsolution for
C
. By the comparison theorem for sub- and supersolutions in the form of
[
12
, Theorem 5.8]
, it follows that
\hat{V}(I,X+\varepsilon\hat{X},Y,\Phi+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi})\geq V^{C+\varepsilon C}
. Moreover, since
V^{C+\varepsilon C}\geq V^{C}
by
[
12
, Proposition 6.8]
,
\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(I,X+\varepsilon\hat{X},Y,\Phi+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi})\geq V^{C}
, and (
A.90
) follows from considering
t=0
and using Corollary
A.6
(b).

Case
\theta>1
.

By Theorem
5.15
and Proposition
A.5
(c), it suffices to show that for each
C\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(x,y,\phi)\cap\mathds{U}\mathds{P}^{*}
,

V^{C}_{0}\leq\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(0,x,y,\phi),

(A.91)

So fix
C\in\mathscr{C}^{(\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow})}(x,y,\phi)\cap\mathds{U}\mathds{P}^{*}
with corresponding investment process
\Phi
and cash process
X=X^{x,y,\phi,\Phi,C}
. Moreover, set
\hat{X}=\hat{X}^{x,y,\phi,\hat{\Phi},\hat{C}}
.
As in the case
\theta\leq 1
, the cases
R<1
and
R>1
require slightly different arguments.

First, assume
R>1
. For each
\varepsilon>0
,
\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(I,X+\varepsilon\hat{X},Y,\Phi+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi})
is a supersolution for the pair
(g_{EZ},C+\varepsilon\hat{C})
by Proposition
5.17
.
Since
e^{-\delta\theta t}(C_{t}+\varepsilon\hat{C}_{t})^{1-R}\leq e^{-\delta\theta t}(\varepsilon\hat{C})^{1-R}
for all
t\geq 0
, it follows from Corollary
A.11
and
[
13
, Proposition E.3]
that there exists a unique extremal solution
V^{C+\varepsilon\hat{C}}
for
(C+\varepsilon\hat{C})
and
V^{C+\varepsilon\hat{C}}
is increasing in
\varepsilon
. It is the minimal supersolution by
[
13
, Corollary E.4]
. Hence, by minimality,
V^{C+\varepsilon\hat{C}}\leq\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(I,X+\varepsilon\hat{X},Y,\Phi+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi})<0
. In particular,
V^{C+\varepsilon\hat{C}}_{0}\leq\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(0,X_{0}+\varepsilon\hat{X}_{0},y,\Phi_{0}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{0})
, and letting
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
and using Proposition
A.5
(c), we obtain

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}V^{C+\varepsilon\hat{C}}_{0}\leq V^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(0,x,y,\phi).

(A.92)

Let
V^{*}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}V^{C+\varepsilon\hat{C}}
. Then
V^{*}_{0}\leq\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(0,X_{0}+\varepsilon\hat{X}_{0},y,\Phi_{0}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{0})
. Consequently, since
g_{EZ}
is increasing in its last two arguments, and
C+\varepsilon\hat{C}
and
V^{C+\varepsilon\hat{C}}
are increasing in
\varepsilon
,
g_{EZ}(\cdot,C+\varepsilon\hat{C},V^{C+\varepsilon\hat{C}})
is increasing in
\varepsilon
. Applying the Monotone Convergence Theorem for conditional expectations yields

V^{*}_{t}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}V^{C+\varepsilon\hat{C}}_{t}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{\infty}g_{EZ}(s,C_{s}+\varepsilon\hat{C}_{s},V^{C+\varepsilon\hat{C}}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{\infty}g_{EZ}(s,C_{s},V^{*}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{t}\right].

Therefore,
V^{*}
is a solution associated to
C
. Moreover, for each
t\geq 0
,
V^{*}_{t}<0

\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}
by the fact that
V^{C+\varepsilon\hat{C}}_{t}<0

\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}
for each
\varepsilon
and
V^{C+\varepsilon\hat{C}}
is increasing in
\varepsilon
. Thus,
V^{*}
is proper. It therefore agrees with the unique proper solution
V^{C}
and via (
A.92
) we obtain (
A.91
).

Next, assume
R<1
. For
\nu>0
and let
C^{\nu}_{t}\coloneqq C_{t}\wedge\nu^{-1}e^{-t}\hat{C}_{t}
. Then
\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(I,X+\varepsilon\hat{X},Y,\Phi+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi})
is a supersolution for
C+\varepsilon\hat{C}
by Proposition
5.17
, and a fortiori a supersolution for
C^{\nu}
.

Moreover, there exists an extremal solution
V^{C^{\nu}}
associated to
C^{\nu}
which is decreasing in
\nu
by
[
13
, Proposition E.3]
. Furthermore, since
C
is right-continuous,
C^{\nu}
is right-continuous. The extremal solution
V^{C^{\nu}}
associated to
C^{\varepsilon}
is therefore proper by
[
13
, Theorem 4.5 and Remark 5.7]
.

Furthermore, using that
(C^{\nu}_{t})^{1-R}\leq\nu^{R-1}e^{(R-1)t}\hat{C_{t}}^{1-R}
, we obtain

\displaystyle(1-R)V^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(t,X_{t}+\varepsilon\hat{X}_{t},Y,\Phi_{t}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{t})

\displaystyle\geq e^{-\delta\theta t}(\nu\hat{C}_{t})^{1-R}n_{\xi}(\hat{Q}_{t})^{-\theta}

(A.93)

\displaystyle\geq\nu^{2(1-R)}n_{\xi}(Q_{t})^{-\theta}e^{(1-R-\delta\theta)t}(C^{\nu}_{t})^{1-R}

(A.94)

\displaystyle\geq\nu^{2(1-R)}D^{-\theta}e^{(1-R-\delta\theta)t}(C^{\nu}_{t})^{1-R},

(A.95)

where
D:=m(q_{*}(\xi))
is an upper bound for
n_{\xi}
.

Now using that

V^{C^{\nu}}
is uniformly integrable by
[
13
, Remark 2.2]
, the comparison result
[
13
, Corollary B.6]
, gives
\hat{V}^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(I,X+\varepsilon\hat{X},Y,\Phi+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi})\geq V^{C^{\nu}}
. In particular,
V^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(0,X_{0}+\varepsilon\hat{X}_{0},y,\Phi_{0}+\varepsilon\hat{\Phi}_{0})\geq V^{C^{\nu}}_{0}
, and letting
\varepsilon,\nu\downarrow 0
and using Proposition
A.6
(b), we obtain

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}V^{C^{\nu}}_{0}\leq V^{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(0,x,y,\phi).

(A.96)

Finally, set
V^{*}=\lim_{\nu\to 0}V^{C^{\nu}}
. Using that
\frac{c^{1-S}}{1-S}((1-R)v)^{\rho}
is increasing both in
c
and
v
(recall
\rho>0
), monotone convergence gives, for
t\geq 0

\displaystyle V^{*}_{t}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-\delta s}\frac{(C^{\nu}_{s})^{1-S}}{1-S}((1-R)V^{C^{\nu}}_{s})^{\rho}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-\delta s}\frac{C_{s}^{1-S}}{1-S}((1-R)V^{*}_{s})^{\rho}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{t}\right].

(A.97)

Hence,
V^{*}
is a solution for
C
. It is a proper solution since for each
t\geq 0
,
V^{*}_{t}>0
if
V^{C^{\nu}}_{t}>0
for some
\nu
and
V^{C^{\nu}}_{t}>0
on
\{J^{(C^{\nu})^{1-R}}>0\}=\{J^{C^{1-R}}>0\}
up to null sets by the fact that each
V^{C^{\nu}}
is proper and
\hat{C}
is strictly positive. Therefore,
V^{*}
must agree with the unique proper solution
V^{C}
associated to
C
, and via (
A.96
) we obtain (
A.91
).
∎

A.5
The ill posed case

Proof of Proposition
5.19
.

Define
\overline{\xi}
as in (
5.1
).

(a) We only provide an argument for the case
S\leq R<1
; the argument for the case
R<S<1
is very similar and hence omitted. We distinguish three cases.

Case 1:
m(0)\leq 0
.
Without loss of generality we may assume that
x>0
and
\phi=0
, since otherwise one can liquidate the entire risky position at
t=0
and end up with a strictly positive pure-cash position, which lies in the interior of the solvency region. Consider a sequence of investment-consumption strategies
(\Phi^{n},C^{n})_{n\in\mathds{N}}
, where
\Phi^{n}\equiv 0
and
C^{n}=\eta_{n}Z^{n}
where
(\eta_{n})_{n\in\mathds{N}}
is a decreasing sequence of positive real numbers converging to
-\frac{S}{1-S}m(0)\geq 0
and
Z^{n}:=Z^{x,\Phi^{n},C^{n}}
; see (
3.1
). It is easy to verify that
C^{n}\in\mathscr{C}_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(x,\phi,y)
for each
n
. Then by the calculations in Section 3.1, the (unique) utility process
V^{C^{n}}=(V^{C^{n}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
associated with
C^{n}
satisfies

V^{C^{n}}_{0}=\left(\frac{\eta_{n}^{1-S}}{H(0,\eta_{n})}\right)^{\theta}\frac{x^{1-R}}{1-R}=\left(\frac{\eta_{n}^{1-S}}{S(m(0)+\frac{1-S}{S}\eta_{n})}\right)^{\theta}\frac{x^{1-R}}{1-R},

where
H(\cdot,\cdot)
is defined in (
3.8
). Now letting
n\to\infty
, the claim follows.

Case 2:
m(0)>0
and
m(1)\leq 0
.
Without loss of generality we may assume that
x=0
and
\phi>0
, since otherwise at
t=0
one can invest all the current wealth into the risky asset and end up with a pure-stock position, which lies in the interior of the solvency region. Consider a sequence of investment-consumption strategies
(\Phi^{n},C^{n})_{n\in\mathds{N}}
, where
\Phi^{n}=\frac{Z^{n}}{Y}
and
C^{n}=\frac{\eta_{n}}{\gamma^{\downarrow}}Z^{n}
where
(\eta_{n})_{n\in\mathds{N}}
is a decreasing sequence of positive real numbers converging to
-\frac{S}{1-S}m(1)\geq 0
and
Z^{n}:=Z^{x,\Phi^{n},C^{n}}
; see (
3.1
). It is easy to verify that
C^{n}\in\mathscr{C}_{\gamma^{\downarrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(x,\phi,y)
for each
n
. Then by similar calculations as in Section 3.1, the (unique) utility process
V^{C^{n}}=(V^{C^{n}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
associated with
C^{n}
satisfies

V^{C^{n}}_{0}=\left(\frac{\eta_{n}^{1-S}}{H(1,\eta_{n})}\right)^{\theta}\frac{\left(\frac{x}{\gamma^{\downarrow}}\right)^{1-R}}{1-R}=\left(\frac{\eta_{n}^{1-S}}{S(m(1)+\frac{1-S}{S}\eta_{n})}\right)^{\theta}\frac{\left(\frac{x}{\gamma^{\downarrow}}\right)^{1-R}}{1-R},

where
H(\cdot,\cdot)
is defined in (
3.8
). Now letting
n\to\infty
, the claim follows.

Case 3:
m(0)>0
,
m(1)>0
,
m(q_{M})<0
and
\xi\leq\bar{\xi}
. Consider a decreasing sequence
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathds{N}}
with
\xi_{n}\downarrow\bar{\xi}
. For each
n\in\mathds{N}
, set
\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n}:=\xi_{n}/\gamma^{\downarrow}
and denote by
(\Phi^{n},C^{n})
the optimal strategy from Theorem
5.13
corresponding to the (well-posed) problem with transaction cost
\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n}
on purchases and
\gamma^{\downarrow}
on sales. Note that each
C^{n}
is
(\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow})
-attainable as
\mathscr{C}_{\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(x,\phi,y)\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(x,\phi,y)
.
Since
\underline{x}:=x+\phi^{+}y\frac{1}{\gamma^{\downarrow}}-\phi^{-}y\gamma^{\uparrow}>0
we have that for large enough
n
,

x+\phi^{+}y\frac{1}{\gamma^{\downarrow}}-\phi^{-}y\gamma_{n}^{\uparrow}>\frac{\underline{x}}{2}>0
and
then using Theorem
5.15
together with the lower bound
x+\phi y\overline{\kappa}_{\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n},\gamma^{\downarrow}}\left(\overline{q}_{\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n},\gamma^{\downarrow}}\left(\frac{\phi y}{x+\phi y}\right)\right)\geq x+\phi^{+}y\frac{1}{\gamma^{\downarrow}}-\phi^{-}y\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n}\geq\frac{\underline{x}}{2}
gives

\displaystyle V^{C^{n}}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Big(x+\phi y\overline{\kappa}_{\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n},\gamma^{\downarrow}}\left(\overline{q}_{\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n},\gamma^{\downarrow}}\left(\frac{\phi y}{x+\phi y}\right)\right)\Big)^{1-R}}{1-R}\overline{n}_{\xi_{n}}\left(\overline{q}_{\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n},\gamma^{\downarrow}}\left(\frac{\phi y}{x+\phi y}\right)\right)^{-\theta S}\geq\frac{{\underline{x}}^{1-R}}{(1-R)2^{1-R}}\left(\|\overline{n}_{\xi_{n}}\|_{\infty}\right)^{-\theta S}

Using that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\overline{n}_{\xi_{n}}\|_{\infty}=0
by Corollary
A.3
, the result follows.

(b) We only provide an argument for the case
S\geq R<1
; the argument for the case
R>S>1
is very similar and hence omitted. We distinguish two cases.

Case 1:
m(q_{M})\leq 0
. Since
\mathscr{C}_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(x,\phi,y)\subseteq\mathscr{C}_{0}(x+\phi,y)
, the claim follows from the fact that for each
C\in\mathscr{C}_{0}(x+\phi,y)
, we have
V^{C}_{0}=-\infty
by
[
12
, Corollary 8.2]
.

Case 2:
m(q_{M})>0
and
\xi\geq\bar{\xi}
.

Note that the finiteness of transaction costs implies
\bar{\xi}<\infty
while
m(q_{M})>0
implies
\bar{\xi}>1
. Let
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathds{N}}
be an increasing sequence with
\xi_{n}\uparrow\bar{\xi}
. For each
n\in\mathds{N}
set
\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n}=\xi_{n}/\gamma^{\downarrow}
. By Theorem
5.18
, the problem with transaction costs
(\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n},\gamma^{\downarrow})
is well-posed for every
n\in\mathds{N}
with value function given by (
5.28
). Fix
C\in\mathscr{C}_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(x,\phi,y)
.
We have the upper bound
x+\phi y\overline{\kappa}_{\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n},\gamma^{\downarrow}}\left(\overline{q}_{\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n},\gamma^{\downarrow}}\left(\frac{\phi y}{x+\phi y}\right)\right)\leq\xi x+\phi^{+}y\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n}-\phi^{-}y\frac{1}{\gamma^{\downarrow}}\leq\xi x+\phi^{+}y\gamma^{\uparrow}-\phi^{-}y\frac{1}{\gamma^{\downarrow}}=\xi\underline{x}

and then, since
\mathscr{C}_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(x,\phi,y)\subset\mathscr{C}_{\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(x,\phi,y)
by Remark
4.3
, Theorem
5.15
gives

\displaystyle V^{C}_{0}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{C\in\mathscr{C}_{\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(x,\phi,y)}V^{C}_{0}=\frac{\Big(x+\phi y\overline{\kappa}_{\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n},\gamma^{\downarrow}}\left(\overline{q}_{\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n},\gamma^{\downarrow}}\left(\frac{\phi y}{x+\phi y}\right)\right)\Big)^{1-R}}{1-R}\overline{n}_{\xi_{i}}\left(\overline{q}_{\gamma^{\uparrow}_{n},\gamma^{\downarrow}}\left(\frac{\phi y}{x+\phi y}\right)\right)^{-\theta S}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\xi\underline{x}\right)^{1-R}}{1-R}\left(\|\overline{n}_{\xi_{n}}\|_{\infty}\right)^{-\theta S}.

By Corollary
A.3
we have that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\overline{n}_{\xi_{n}}\|_{\infty}=0
and the result follows.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs for small transaction costs

Proof of Proposition
7.1
.

We consider the case that
q_{M}=\frac{\lambda}{\sigma R}\in(0,\infty)\setminus\{1\}
; the case
q_{M}<0
can be treated in a similar way. The case
q_{M}=1
is broadly similar, but the leading terms are of different order. See Hobson et al
[
15
]
for a derivation of the expansion for the case
q_{M}=1
in the additive setting.

By Definition
5.1
, the assumptions that the problem (
4.3
) is well-posed for arbitrarily small transaction cost implies that
\hat{m}=m(q_{M})\geq 0
if
R<1
and
m(q_{M})>0
if
R>1
.

Recall
n^{+}_{(z)}
solves
n^{\prime}=O(q,n)
subject to
n^{+}_{(z)}(z)=m(z)
. (Here
z\in(0,q_{M})\cap\{q:m(q)>0\}
.) Then
\zeta^{+}=\zeta^{+}(z)>q_{M}
is such that
n^{+}_{(z)}(\zeta^{+})=m(\zeta^{+})
and moreover
(1-R)n^{+}_{(z)}>(1-R)m
on
(z,\zeta^{+})\setminus\{1\}
. Further,
\Sigma^{+}(z)=\exp\left(\int_{z}^{\zeta^{+}(z)}\frac{1}{q(1-q)}\frac{n^{+}_{(z)}(q)-m(q)}{\ell(q)-n^{+}_{(z)}(q)}dq\right)
.

For
u\in(0,q_{M})\cap\{q:m(q)>0\}
introduce
N_{u}:[-u,\infty)\to\mathds{R}^{+}
defined by
N_{u}(v)=n^{+}_{(q_{M}-u)}(q_{M}+v)-m_{M}
. Introduce also
M
and
L
given by
M(v)=m(q_{M}+v)-m_{M}
and
L(v)=\ell(q_{M}+v)-m_{M}
. Note that
M(v)=\frac{R(1-S)}{2S}\sigma^{2}v^{2}=RDv^{2}
where
D=\frac{(1-S)}{2S}\sigma^{2}
.

Define
G_{u}:[-1,\infty)
by
G_{u}(v)=\frac{1}{D}(N_{u}(uv)-M(uv))
. (The normalisation
\frac{1}{D}
is largely cosmetic but will help highlight the point at which the elasticity of intertemporal consumption
S
enters the expansion.) Then
G_{u}(-1)=0
. Further, for
v\geq-1
,

\displaystyle G_{u}^{\prime}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{D}uO(q_{M}+uv,DG_{u}(v)+M(uv)+m_{M})-\frac{1}{D}uM^{\prime}(uv)

(B.1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}u\frac{D(G_{u}(v)+Ru^{2}v^{2})+m_{M}}{1-q_{M}-vu}\frac{G_{u}(v)}{(q_{M}(1-q_{M})+(1-2q_{M})uv-u^{2}v^{2}-G_{u}(v))}-2Rvu^{2}

We look for a solution to (
B.1
) of the form
G_{u}(v)=\sum_{k\geq 0}u^{k}g_{k}(v)
defined for
v\geq-1
. We find that the zeroth order term
g_{0}
satisfies

g^{\prime}_{0}(v)=0\hskip 56.9055ptg_{0}(-1)=0

and conclude that
g_{0}(v)=0
. Similarly, using that
g_{0}\equiv 0
we find

g^{\prime}_{1}(v)=0\hskip 56.9055ptg_{1}(-1)=0

so that again
g_{1}\equiv 0
.
The next three terms (clearly, the expansion method can be continued to arbitrary order) solve

g^{\prime}_{2}(v)=-2Rv

g_{2}(-1)=0

g^{\prime}_{3}(v)=-\frac{2m_{M}}{\sigma^{2}q_{M}(1-q_{M})^{2}}g_{2}(v)

g_{3}(-1)=0

g^{\prime}_{4}(v)=-\frac{2m_{M}}{\sigma^{2}q_{M}(1-q_{M})^{2}}\left(g_{3}(v)+vg_{2}(v)\frac{(3q_{M}-1)}{q_{M}(1-q_{M})}\right)

g_{4}(-1)=0

and we find

\displaystyle g_{2}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R(1-v^{2})

\displaystyle g_{3}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-A(1+v)^{2}(2-v)

\displaystyle g_{4}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B_{1}(1+v)^{3}(3-v)+B_{2}(1-v^{2})^{2}

where

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2R}{3\sigma^{2}}\frac{m_{M}}{q_{M}(1-q_{M})^{2}}

\displaystyle B_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{R}{3\sigma^{4}}\frac{m_{M}^{2}}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{4}}

\displaystyle B_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{R}{2\sigma^{2}}\frac{m_{M}(3q_{M}-1)}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{3}}

Note that if we truncate the expansion at the second order term (i.e. take only the non-zero first term of the expansion the we find
G_{u}(v)\approx u^{2}(1-v^{2})R
so that
N_{u}(uv)\approx u^{2}RD
which is independent of
v
. It follows that for
z
close to
q_{M}
,
n^{+}_{(z)}(q)=m(z)
and then
\zeta^{+}(z)=2q_{M}-z
, and to leading order (in the shadow fraction of wealth co-ordinates) the no-transaction region is symmetric about the Merton ratio.

The expansion for
G_{u}
takes the form

G_{u}(v)=R(1-v^{2})u^{2}-A(1+v)^{2}(2-v)u^{3}+\left[B_{1}(1+v)^{3}(3-v)+B_{2}(1-v^{2})^{2}\right]u^{4}+\mathcal{O}(u^{5}),

and if
\zeta_{(u)}=\inf\{v>-1:G_{u}(v)=0\}
then
\zeta_{(u)}
is approximately 1. We look for a root of the form
\zeta_{(u)}=1-\zeta_{1}u+\zeta_{2}u^{2}+\mathcal{O}(u^{3})
. Then
from
G_{u}(\zeta_{(u)})=0
we find

0=(2R\zeta_{1}-4A)u^{3}+(-2R\zeta_{2}-\zeta_{1}^{2}R+16B_{1})u^{4}+O(u^{5})

It follows that

\zeta_{1}=\frac{2A}{R}=\frac{4m_{M}}{3\sigma^{2}q_{M}(1-q_{M})^{2}},\hskip 56.9055pt\zeta_{2}=\zeta_{1}^{2}.

We can now fix
u=q_{M}-q_{*}
via the identity

\displaystyle\ln(\xi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u\int_{-1}^{1-\zeta_{1}u+\zeta_{1}^{2}u^{2}+\mathcal{O}(u^{3})}dv\frac{1}{(q_{M}+uv)(1-q_{M}-uv)}\frac{G_{u}(v)}{(q_{M}+uv)(1-q_{M}-vu)-G_{u}(v)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1-\zeta_{1}u+\zeta_{1}^{2}u^{2}+\mathcal{O}(u^{3})}\frac{dv}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{2}}u\left(1+\frac{uv}{q_{M}}\right)^{-1}\left(1-\frac{uv}{1-q_{M}}\right)^{-1}G_{u}(v)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times\left(\left(1+\frac{uv}{q_{M}}\right)\left(1-\frac{uv}{1-q_{M}}\right)-\frac{G_{u}(v)}{q_{M}(1-q_{M})}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1-\zeta_{1}u+\zeta_{1}^{2}u^{2}+\mathcal{O}(u^{3})}\frac{dv}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times\left(g_{2}(v)u^{3}+\left(g_{3}(v)+\frac{2(2q_{M}-1)}{q_{M}(1-q_{M})}vg_{2}(v)\right)u^{4}+\Psi(v)u^{5}+\mathcal{O}(u^{6})\right)

where

\Psi(v)=g_{4}(v)+\frac{2(2q_{M}-1)}{q_{M}(1-q_{M})}vg_{3}(v)+\frac{1}{q_{M}(1-q_{M})}g_{2}(v)^{2}+\frac{3-10q_{M}+10q_{M}^{2}}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{2}}v^{2}g_{2}(v)

Now
\int_{-1}^{z}(1-v^{2})dv=\frac{1}{3}(z+1)^{2}(2-z)
so that

\int_{-1}^{1-\zeta_{1}u+\zeta_{1}^{2}u^{2}+\mathcal{O}(u^{3})}g_{2}(v)dv=\frac{R}{3}(4-3\zeta_{1}^{2}u^{2})+\mathcal{O}(u^{3})

Similarly,
\int_{-1}^{z}(1+v)^{2}(2-v)dv=\frac{1}{4}(z+1)^{3}(3-z)
and
\int_{-1}^{z}v(1-v^{2})dv=-\frac{1}{4}(z^{2}-1)^{2}
so that

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1-\zeta_{1}u+\mathcal{O}(u^{2})}\left(g_{3}(v)+\frac{2(2q_{M}-1)}{q_{M}(1-q_{M})}vg_{2}(v)\right)dv

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-4A(1-\zeta_{1}u)+\mathcal{O}(u^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{8R}{3\sigma^{2}}\frac{m_{M}}{q_{M}(1-q_{M})^{2}}+\left(\frac{8R}{3\sigma^{2}}\frac{m_{M}}{q_{M}(1-q_{M})^{2}}\right)\zeta_{1}u+\mathcal{O}(u^{2})

Finally,
\int_{-1}^{1}(1+v)^{3}(3-v)dv=\frac{48}{5}
,
\int_{-1}^{1}(1-v^{2})^{2}dv=\frac{16}{15}
,
\int_{-1}^{1}v(1+v)^{2}(2-v)dv=\frac{8}{5}
,
\int_{-1}^{1}v^{2}(1-v^{2})dv=\frac{4}{15}
so that

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1+\mathcal{O}(u)}\Psi(v)dv

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{16}{5}\frac{R}{\sigma^{4}}\frac{m_{M}^{2}}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{4}}+\frac{8R}{15\sigma^{2}}\frac{m_{M}(3q_{M}-1)}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{3}}-\frac{32R}{15\sigma^{2}}\frac{m_{M}(2q_{M}-1)}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{3}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{16}{15}\frac{1}{q_{M}(1-q_{M})}R^{2}+\frac{4}{15}\frac{(3-10q_{M}+10q_{M}^{2})}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{2}}R+\mathcal{O}(u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{16R}{5\sigma^{4}}\frac{m_{M}^{2}}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{4}}-\frac{8R}{15\sigma^{2}}m_{M}\frac{(5q_{M}-3)}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{3}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{16}{15}\frac{1}{q_{M}(1-q_{M})}R^{2}+\frac{4}{15}\frac{(3-10q_{M}+10q_{M}^{2})}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{2}}R+\mathcal{O}(u)

Putting this all together, writing
\xi=1+\varepsilon
, and then using the expression for
\zeta_{1}
,

\displaystyle\ln(1+\varepsilon)\left(=\varepsilon-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3})=\varepsilon^{\uparrow}+\varepsilon^{\downarrow}-\frac{(\varepsilon^{\uparrow})^{2}-(\varepsilon^{\downarrow})^{2}}{2}++\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4R}{3q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{2}}u^{3}-\frac{8R}{3\sigma^{2}}\frac{m_{M}}{q_{M}^{3}(1-q_{M})^{4}}u^{4}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{R}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{2}}\left(-\zeta_{1}^{2}+\frac{8}{3\sigma^{2}}\frac{m_{M}\zeta_{1}}{q_{M}(1-q_{M})^{2}}+\frac{4}{15}\frac{(3-10q_{M}+10q_{M}^{2})}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{2}}+\frac{16}{15}R\frac{1}{q_{M}(1-q_{M})}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\left.\frac{16}{5\sigma^{4}}\frac{m_{M}^{2}}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{4}}-\frac{8}{15\sigma^{2}}m_{M}\frac{(5q_{M}-3)}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{3}}\right)+\mathcal{O}(u^{6})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4R}{3q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{2}}u^{3}-\frac{2R}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{2}}\zeta_{1}u^{4}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{R}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{2}}\left(\frac{14}{5}\zeta_{1}^{2}-\frac{2}{5}\frac{(5q_{M}-3)}{q_{M}(1-q_{M})}\zeta_{1}+\frac{4}{15}\frac{(3-10q_{M}+10q_{M}^{2})}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{2}}+\frac{16}{15}R\frac{1}{q_{M}(1-q_{M})}\right)u^{5}+\mathcal{O}(u^{6})

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle C_{3}u^{3}+C_{4}u^{4}+C_{5}u^{5}+\mathcal{O}(u^{6})

Comparing the two sides of the equation we see that for the equation to balance we must have that
u=q_{M}-q_{*}(1+\varepsilon)=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{1/3})
.
We look for an expressing for
q_{*}
of the form

u=\Delta^{\frac{1}{3}}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{3}}+\Sigma\Delta^{\frac{2}{3}}\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}}+\Psi\Delta\varepsilon+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{4}{3}}\right)

or equivalently

q_{*}(1+\varepsilon)=q_{M}-\Delta^{\frac{1}{3}}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{3}}-\Sigma\Delta^{\frac{2}{3}}\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}}-\Psi\Delta\varepsilon+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{4}{3}}\right)

Then

\varepsilon=C_{3}\left(\Delta\varepsilon+3\Sigma(\Delta\varepsilon)^{4/3}+3(\Sigma^{2}+\Psi)(\Delta\varepsilon)^{5/3}\right)+C_{4}\left((\Delta\varepsilon)^{4/3}+4\Sigma(\Delta\varepsilon)^{5/3}\right)+C_{5}(\Delta\varepsilon)^{5/3}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

The constants
\Sigma
,
\Delta
and
\Psi
can be fixed by the identities
1=C_{3}\Delta
,
0=C_{4}+3\Sigma C_{3}
and
0=3(\Sigma^{2}+\Psi)C_{3}+4\Sigma C_{4}+C_{5}
.
We find

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{3}}=\frac{3q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{2}}{4R}

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{C_{4}}{3C_{3}}=\frac{2m_{M}}{3\sigma^{2}q_{M}(1-q_{M})^{2}}=\frac{\zeta_{1}}{2}

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{C_{5}+4\Sigma C_{4}}{3C_{3}}-\Sigma^{2}=3\Sigma^{2}-\frac{C_{5}}{3C_{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{5}\Sigma^{2}+\frac{1}{5}\frac{(5q_{M}-3)}{q_{M}(1-q_{M})}\Sigma-\frac{(3-10q_{M}+10q_{M}^{2})+4Rq_{M}(1-q_{M})}{15q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{2}}

Further,

\displaystyle q^{*}(1+\varepsilon)-q_{M}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u(\varepsilon)\zeta_{(u(\varepsilon))}=u(\varepsilon)(1-\zeta_{1}u(\varepsilon)+\zeta_{1}^{2}u(\varepsilon)^{2})+\mathcal{O}(u(\varepsilon)^{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta^{\frac{1}{3}}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{3}}+\Sigma\Delta^{\frac{2}{3}}\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}}+\Psi\Delta\varepsilon-\zeta_{1}\left(\Delta^{\frac{2}{3}}\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}}+2\Sigma\Delta\varepsilon\right)+\zeta_{1}^{2}\Delta\varepsilon+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4/3})

It follows that

q^{*}(1+\varepsilon)=q_{M}+\Delta^{\frac{1}{3}}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{3}}-\Sigma\Delta^{\frac{2}{3}}\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}}+\Psi\Delta\varepsilon+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{4}{3}}\right)\qed

Proof of Corollary
7.3
.

Setting
\varepsilon:=(1+\varepsilon^{\uparrow})(1+\varepsilon^{\downarrow})-1
and using (
5.18
) and (
5.19
), we obtain

\displaystyle p_{*}(1+\varepsilon^{\uparrow},1+\varepsilon^{\downarrow})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{q_{*}(1+\varepsilon)}{1+\varepsilon^{\uparrow}(1-q_{*}(1+\varepsilon))}=q_{*}(1+\varepsilon)-\varepsilon^{\uparrow}q_{*}(1+\varepsilon)(1-q_{*}(1+\varepsilon))+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle p^{*}(1+\varepsilon^{\uparrow},1+\varepsilon^{\downarrow})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1+\varepsilon^{\downarrow})q^{*}(1+\varepsilon)}{1+\varepsilon^{\downarrow}q^{*}(1+\varepsilon)}=q^{*}(1+\varepsilon)+\varepsilon^{\downarrow}q^{*}(1+\varepsilon)(1-q^{*}(1+\varepsilon))+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

Now the result follows from Proposition
7.5
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.5
.

Setting
\gamma^{\uparrow}:=1+\varepsilon^{\uparrow}
,
\gamma^{\downarrow}:=1+\varepsilon^{\downarrow}
,
\xi:=\gamma^{\uparrow}\gamma^{\downarrow}
and
\varepsilon:=\xi-1
, and
looking at the expression (
4.31
) for the optimal consumption, we need to consider how
\kappa(q(p))=\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q(p))
and
n_{\xi}(q(p))
vary with
\varepsilon^{\uparrow}
and
\varepsilon^{\downarrow}
as functions of
p
.

Set
u=u(\varepsilon)=q_{M}-q_{*}(1+\varepsilon)
. Recalling that
\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}:[q_{*}(\xi),q^{*}(\xi)]\to[\frac{1}{\gamma^{\downarrow}},\gamma^{\uparrow}]
is monotonically decreasing and hence
\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q(p))=1+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
, set

\tilde{\kappa}_{u}(v)=\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q_{M}+uv)=\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q_{M}+(q_{M}-q_{*}(1+\varepsilon))v).

Then
\tilde{\kappa}_{u}(-1)=\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q_{*}(\xi))=\gamma^{\uparrow}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{\kappa}_{u}^{\prime}(v)}{\tilde{\kappa}_{u}(v)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u\frac{\kappa^{\prime}_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q_{M}+uv)}{\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q_{M}+uv)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{u}{(q_{M}+uv)(1-q_{M}-uv)}\frac{(n^{+}_{(q_{*}(1+\varepsilon))}(q_{M}+uv)-m(q_{M}+uv))}{(\ell(q_{M}+uv)-n^{+}_{(q_{*}(1+\varepsilon))}(q_{M}+uv))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{u}{(q_{M}+uv)(1-q_{M}-uv)}\frac{N_{u}(uv)-M(uv)}{L(uv)-N_{u}(uv)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{u}{(q_{M}+uv)(1-q_{M}-uv)}\frac{DG_{u}(v)}{D(q_{M}+uv)(1-q_{M}-uv)-DG_{u}(v)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{u^{3}g_{2}(v)}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(u^{4})

We find, with
h_{3}(v)=(1+v)^{2}(2-v)=3\int_{-1}^{v}(1-w^{2})dw
, for
v\in[-1,\zeta_{(u)}]
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\kappa}_{u}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma^{\uparrow}\exp\left(-\frac{u^{3}}{q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{2}}\int_{-1}^{v}g_{2}(w)dw+\mathcal{O}(u^{4})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma^{\uparrow}\exp\left(-\frac{R\varepsilon\Delta}{3q_{M}^{2}(1-q_{M})^{2}}h_{3}(v)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4/3})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma^{\uparrow}\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}h_{3}(v)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4/3})\right)

Note that
\kappa_{u}(1)=\gamma^{\uparrow}\exp\left(-\varepsilon\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4/3})=\gamma^{\uparrow}\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4/3})=\frac{1}{\gamma^{\downarrow}}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4/3})
.

Since
\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q(p))=1+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
it follows that
p(q)=\frac{q}{1-q(1-\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q))}=q+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
which can be inverted to give
q(p)=p+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
. Further,
q
and
q
both lie in intervals of width of order
\varepsilon^{1/3}
around
q_{M}
, so
q(p)=q_{M}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{1/3})
. It follows that, with
p=\frac{y\phi}{x+y\phi}
and
v
as shorthand for
\frac{p-q_{M}}{u}
in later lines,

\displaystyle x+y\phi\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q(p))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x+y\phi)\left(1+p(\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(q(p))-1)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x+y\phi)\left(1+q_{M}(\kappa_{\gamma^{\uparrow},\gamma^{\downarrow}}(p)-1)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4/3})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x+y\phi)\left(1+q_{M}(\tilde{\kappa}_{u}(v)-1)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4/3})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x+y\phi)\left(1+q_{M}\left(\gamma^{\uparrow}\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}h_{3}(v)\right)-1\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4/3})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x+y\phi)\left(1+q_{M}\left((1+\varepsilon^{\uparrow})\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon^{\uparrow}+\varepsilon^{\downarrow}}{4}h_{3}(v)\right)-1\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4/3})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x+y\phi)\left(1+q_{M}\left(1-\frac{h_{3}(v)}{4}\right)\varepsilon^{\uparrow}-q_{M}\frac{h_{3}(v)}{4}\varepsilon^{\downarrow}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4/3})

Now we turn to the expansion of
n_{\xi}(q(p))
.
We have
n_{\xi}(q(p))=n^{+}_{(q_{M}-u(\varepsilon))}(q(p))
.
Then, using
g_{3}=-Ah_{3}
we have

\displaystyle n^{+}_{(q_{M}-u(\varepsilon))}(q(p))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{M}+N_{u(\varepsilon)}(q(p)-q_{M})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{M}+M(q(p)-q_{M})+DG_{u(\varepsilon)}\left(\frac{(q(p)-q_{M})}{u(\varepsilon)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{M}+RD(q(p)-q_{M})^{2}+Du(\varepsilon)^{2}g_{2}\left(\frac{(q(p)-q_{M})}{u(\varepsilon)}\right)+Du(\varepsilon)^{3}g_{3}\left(\frac{(q(p)-q_{M})}{u(\varepsilon)}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4/3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{M}+\frac{R(1-S)}{S}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}u(\varepsilon)^{2}-DAu(\varepsilon)^{3}h_{3}\left(\frac{(q(p)-q_{M})}{u(\varepsilon)}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4/3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{M}\left(1+\frac{R(1-S)}{S}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2m_{M}}\left(\Delta^{2/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}+2\Delta\Sigma\varepsilon\right)-\frac{DA\Delta}{m_{M}}\varepsilon h_{3}\left(\frac{p-q_{M}}{\Delta^{1/3}\varepsilon^{1/3}}\right)\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4/3})

Note that
\frac{DA\Delta}{m_{M}}
can be written more simply as
\frac{q_{M}}{4}\frac{1-S}{S}
and
\frac{R\Delta\Sigma\sigma^{2}}{\hat{m}}=\frac{q_{M}}{2}
. Hence this expression simplifies to

n^{+}_{(q_{M}-u(\varepsilon))}(q(p))=m_{M}\left(1+\frac{R(1-S)}{S}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2m_{M}}\Delta^{2/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}+q_{M}\frac{(1-S)}{2S}\left(1-\frac{1}{2}h_{3}\left(\frac{p-q_{M}}{\Delta^{1/3}\varepsilon^{1/3}}\right)\right)\varepsilon\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4/3}).

Putting this all together and using (
4.31
),

\displaystyle C^{*}_{1+\varepsilon^{\uparrow},1+\varepsilon^{\downarrow}}(t,x,\phi,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x+y\phi)m_{M}\left(1+q_{M}\left(1-\frac{1}{4}h_{3}\left(\frac{p-q_{M}}{\Delta^{1/3}\varepsilon^{1/3}}\right)\right)\varepsilon^{\uparrow}-q_{M}\frac{1}{4}h_{3}\left(\frac{p-q_{M}}{\Delta^{1/3}\varepsilon^{1/3}}\right)\varepsilon^{\downarrow}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\left(1+\frac{R(1-S)}{S}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2m_{M}}\Delta^{2/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}+q_{M}\frac{(1-S)}{2S}\left(1-\frac{1}{2}h_{3}\left(\frac{p-q_{M}}{\Delta^{1/3}\varepsilon^{1/3}}\right)\right)\varepsilon\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4/3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x+y\phi)m_{M}\left(1+\frac{R(1-S)}{S}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2m_{M}}\Delta^{2/3}(\varepsilon^{\uparrow}+\varepsilon^{\downarrow})^{2/3}+\frac{q_{M}}{2S}\left(1-\frac{1}{2}h_{3}\left(\frac{p-q_{M}}{\Delta^{1/3}\varepsilon^{1/3}}\right)\right)(\varepsilon^{\uparrow}+\varepsilon^{\downarrow})+\frac{q_{M}}{2}(\varepsilon^{\uparrow}-\varepsilon^{\downarrow})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4/3})

∎

Appendix C
Proofs for the results on comparative statics

C.1
Comparative statics in S

Proof of Proposition
8.1
.

We will establish the ordering of the optimal purchase/sale boundaries in the
q
-coordinate, which can then be extended to that of
\hat{p}_{*}^{*}
and
\tilde{p}_{*}^{*}
by the monotonicity of the Möbius transformation over the relevant domain.

Let

\overline{m}(q):=\frac{S}{1-S}\left(m(q)-\frac{\delta}{S}\right)=-r-\lambda\sigma q+\frac{R\sigma^{2}}{2}q^{2}

which is independent of
S
. Similarly, let

\overline{\ell}(q):=\frac{S}{1-S}\left(\ell(q)-\frac{\delta}{S}\right)=-r-\left(\lambda\sigma-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\right)q-(1-R)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}q^{2}

which is also independent of
S
.

Suppose
(n(\cdot),q_{*},q^{*})
is the solution to the free boundary problem in Proposition
5.3
. Then
n^{\prime}(q)=O(q,n(q))
on
q\in[q_{*},q^{*}]
,
n(q_{*})=m(q_{*})
,
n(q^{*})=m(q^{*})
, and

\displaystyle\ln\gamma^{\uparrow}\gamma^{\downarrow}=\ln\xi=\int_{q_{*}}^{q^{*}}\frac{1}{q(1-q)}\frac{n(q)-m(q)}{\ell(q)-n(q)}\,\mathrm{d}q.

Define
\bar{n}(q):=\frac{S}{1-S}\left(n(q)-\frac{\delta}{S}\right)
. We have

\displaystyle\bar{n}^{\prime}(q)=\frac{S}{1-S}n^{\prime}(q)=\frac{S}{1-S}O(q,n(q))

\displaystyle=\frac{S}{1-S}O(q,(1-S)\bar{n}(q)/S+\delta/S)

\displaystyle=\frac{\bar{n}(q)}{1-q}\frac{\bar{n}(q)-\bar{m}(q)}{\bar{\ell}(q)-\bar{n}(q)}-\frac{1}{S}\frac{1}{1-q}\frac{\bar{n}(q)-\bar{m}(q)}{\bar{\ell}(q)-\bar{n}(q)}\left(\bar{n}(q)+\delta\right)

\displaystyle=\bar{O}(q,\bar{n}(q))

over
q\in[q_{*},q^{*}]
, where

\bar{O}(q,\bar{n}):=\frac{\bar{n}}{1-q}\frac{\bar{n}-\bar{m}(q)}{\bar{\ell}(q)-\bar{n}}-\frac{1}{S}\frac{1}{1-q}\frac{\bar{n}-\bar{m}(q)}{\bar{\ell}(q)-\bar{n}}\left(\bar{n}+\delta\right).

The boundary conditions at
q=q^{*}_{*}
can be restated as
\bar{n}(q_{*})=\bar{m}(q_{*})
and
\bar{n}(q^{*})=\bar{m}(q^{*})
. The integral constraint becomes

\displaystyle\ln\xi=\int_{q_{*}}^{q^{*}}\frac{1}{q(1-q)}\frac{\bar{n}(q)-\bar{m}(q)}{\bar{\ell}(q)-\bar{n}(q)}\,\mathrm{d}q.

Finally, away from
1
, the solution trajectory
n=(n(q))_{q_{*}\leq q\leq q^{*}}
always satisfies
\frac{1}{1-q}\frac{n(q)-m(q)}{\ell(q)-n(q)}\sgn(q_{M})\geq 0
and in turn
\frac{1}{1-q}\frac{\bar{n}(q)-\bar{m}(q)}{\bar{\ell}(q)-\bar{n}(q)}\sgn(q_{M})\geq 0
.

(a)

If (
8.1
) holds, then

\displaystyle\bar{n}(q)+\delta=\frac{S(n(q)-\delta)}{1-S}>\frac{S(m_{M}-\delta)}{1-S}=\delta-r-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2R}\geq 0

as
\sgn(1-S)n(q)>\sgn(1-S)m_{M}
. If we view
\bar{O}(q,\bar{n})=\bar{O}(q,\bar{n};S)
as a function of
S
, then clearly
\bar{O}
is strictly increasing (resp. decreasing) in
S
over
(q,\bar{n})
on which
\frac{1}{1-q}\frac{\bar{n}-\bar{m}(q)}{\bar{\ell}(q)-\bar{n}}\left(\bar{n}+\delta\right)
is positive (resp. negative). Hence given (
8.1
), we must have
\sgn(q_{M})\bar{O}(q,\overline{n}(q);\hat{S})>\sgn(q_{M})\bar{O}(q,\overline{n}(q);\tilde{S})
over
q\in(q_{*},q^{*})\setminus\{1\}
for
\hat{S}>\tilde{S}
.

Recall the definitions of
(n^{+}_{(z)}(q))_{q\in[z,\zeta^{+}(q)]}
,
\zeta^{+}(z)
and
\Sigma^{+}(z)
as well as the relevant domain of
z
defined in Proposition
A.1
or
A.2
. Define
(a^{+}_{(z)}(q))_{q\in[z,\zeta^{+}(z)]}
via
a^{+}_{(z)}(q):=\frac{S}{1-S}(n^{+}_{(z)}(q)-\frac{\delta}{S})
. By construction,

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}q}a^{+}_{(z)}(q)=\bar{O}(q,a^{+}_{(z)}(q);S),\hskip 20.00003ptq\in[z,\zeta^{+}(z)],

a^{+}_{(z)}(z)=\bar{m}(z)
,
a^{+}_{(z)}(\zeta^{+}(z))=\bar{m}(\zeta^{+}(z))
,
and

\Sigma^{+}(z)=\exp\left(\int_{z}^{\zeta^{+}(z)}\frac{1}{q(1-q)}\frac{a^{+}_{(z)}(q)-\bar{m}(q)}{\bar{\ell}(q)-a^{+}_{(z)}(q)}\,\mathrm{d}q\right).

Let
\hat{n}_{(z)}^{+}
and
\tilde{n}_{(z)}^{+}
be the two versions of
n^{+}_{(z)}
under
S=\hat{S}
and
S=\tilde{S}<\hat{S}
respectively while all other model parameters are fixed. We will apply the same overhead notations to
\zeta^{+}(z)
,
a^{+}_{(z)}
and
\Sigma^{+}
which are defined relative to the underlying parameters
\hat{S}
and
\tilde{S}
. Since
\bar{m}(\cdot)
does not depend on
S
,
\hat{a}^{+}_{(z)}(z)=\bar{m}(z)=\tilde{a}^{+}_{(z)}(z)
. Also
(\hat{a}^{+}_{(z)})^{\prime}(z)=0=(\tilde{a}^{+}_{(z)})^{\prime}(z)
for
z\neq 1
.

Suppose that
q_{M}\in(0,1)
. We first show that
\hat{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)>\tilde{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)
near
q=z
for any
z\in(0,q_{M})
. Using the ODE satisfied by
a^{+}_{(z)}
and L’Hospital rule, we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{q\downarrow z}\frac{a^{+}_{(z)}(q)-\bar{m}(z)}{(q-z)^{2}}=\frac{\bar{m}^{\prime}(z)}{\sigma^{2}z(1-z)^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{S}(\bar{m}(z)+\delta)-\bar{m}(z)\right],

where the leading factor is negative and in turn the whole expression is strictly increasing in
S
as
\bar{m}(z)+\delta=\frac{S(m(z)-\delta)}{1-S}>\frac{S(m_{M}-\delta)}{1-S}=\delta-r-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2R}\geq 0
for all
z\neq q_{M}
under condition (
8.1
). Hence
\hat{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)
must be initially strictly larger than
\tilde{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)
. Together with the facts that
\bar{O}(q,a;\hat{S})>\bar{O}(q,a;\tilde{S})
along any solution trajectory (excluding the end points), we conclude
\hat{\zeta}^{+}(z)\geq\tilde{\zeta}^{+}(z)
and
\hat{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)\geq\tilde{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)
at least up to
q=\tilde{\zeta}^{+}(z)
<1. Then

\displaystyle\hat{\Sigma}^{+}(z)=\exp\left(\int_{z}^{\hat{\zeta}^{+}(z)}\frac{1}{q(1-q)}\frac{\hat{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)-\bar{m}(q)}{\bar{\ell}(q)-\hat{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)}\,\mathrm{d}q\right)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(\int_{z}^{\tilde{\zeta}^{+}(z)}\frac{1}{q(1-q)}\frac{\hat{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)-\bar{m}(q)}{\bar{\ell}(q)-\hat{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)}\,\mathrm{d}q\right)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(\int_{z}^{\tilde{\zeta}^{+}(z)}\frac{1}{q(1-q)}\frac{\tilde{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)-\bar{m}(q)}{\bar{\ell}(q)-\tilde{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)}\,\mathrm{d}q\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\Sigma}^{+}(z),

and in turn
\hat{q}_{*}=(\hat{\Sigma}^{+})^{-1}(\xi)\geq(\tilde{\Sigma}^{+})^{-1}(\xi)=\tilde{q}_{*}
since
\Sigma^{+}(\cdot)
is decreasing.

The arguments are similar in the case of
q_{M}>1
except some extra care is needed around
q=1
. For any
z\in(0,1)
,
a^{+}_{(z)}(1)=\bar{m}(1)
and
(a^{+}_{(z)})^{\prime}(1)=\bar{m}^{\prime}(1)
such that the ordering of
\hat{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)
and
\tilde{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)
is not immediately clear on
q>1
. But again we can deduce via L’Hospital rule that

\displaystyle\lim_{q\to 1}\frac{a^{+}_{(z)}(q)-\bar{m}(q)}{(1-q)^{2}}=-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\frac{\bar{m}^{\prime}(1)}{\frac{1}{S}(\bar{m}(1)+\delta)-\bar{m}(1)},

which is positive and strictly increasing in
S
under condition (
8.1
). Hence we conclude
\hat{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)>\tilde{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)
in the neighbourhood of
q=1
.

The results for
q^{*}
can be established similarly by parameterising the solutions to the family of ODEs via their right boundary points. Results for the case of
q_{M}<0
can be derived using similar ideas.

(b)

If instead the parameters are such that (
8.2
) holds, then the argument in part (a) does not work as
\bar{n}(q)+\delta
may change sign and hence the monotonicity of
\bar{O}(q,\bar{n};S)
in
S
is ambiguous. But suppose
q_{M}>0
and the problems under
S\in\{\hat{S},\tilde{S}\}
are well-posed for all sufficiently small transaction costs. Then there exists
\delta>0
such that for all
z\in(q_{M}-\delta,q_{M})
,
n^{+}_{(z)}(q)
is well-defined and is strictly positive over
q\in[z,\zeta^{+}(z)]
. Moreover, since the family of solutions
(n^{+}_{(z)}(\cdot))_{z}
is monotonic in
z
with
n^{+}_{(z)}(q)\to m_{M}
as
z\to q_{M}
, one can choose
\Delta
such that
|n^{+}_{(z)}(q)-m_{M}|<\omega
and
|m(q)-m_{M}|<\omega
on
q\in[z,\zeta^{+}(z)]
for all
z\in(q_{M}-\Delta,q_{M})
where
\omega>0
is arbitrary. Then

\displaystyle a^{+}_{(z)}(q)+\delta=\frac{S(n^{+}_{(z)}(q)-\delta)}{1-S}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{S(m_{M}+\sgn(1-S)\omega-\delta)}{1-S}

\displaystyle=\delta-r-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2R}+\frac{S}{|1-S|}\omega<0

and similarly
\bar{m}(q)+\delta<0
if
\omega
is chosen to be sufficiently small. In such case,
\bar{O}(q,a^{+}_{(z)}(q);\hat{S})<\bar{O}(q,a^{+}_{(z)}(q);\tilde{S})
along the solution trajectory
(a^{+}_{(z)}(q))_{z<q<\zeta^{+}(z)}
, and that
\hat{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)<\tilde{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)
near
q=z
(and near
q=1
as well if
q_{M}>1
). Following similar arguments in part (a), we can conclude
\hat{\zeta}^{+}(z)\leq\tilde{\zeta}^{+}(z)
and
\hat{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)\leq\tilde{a}^{+}_{(z)}(q)
up to
q=\hat{\zeta}^{+}(z)
, and in turn
\hat{\Sigma}^{+}(z)\leq\tilde{\Sigma}^{+}(z)
for
z\in(q_{M}-\delta,q_{M})
. Thus there exists
\varepsilon:=\hat{\Sigma}^{+}(q_{M}-\Delta)-1>0
such that
\hat{q}_{*}=(\hat{\Sigma}^{+})^{-1}(\xi)\leq(\tilde{\Sigma}^{+})^{-1}(\xi)=\tilde{q}_{*}
for all
\xi\leq 1+\varepsilon
.

The proof for
q^{*}
is similar upon parameterising the solutions to the family of ODEs via the right boundary points. The analysis can be analogously extended to
q_{M}<0
.

∎

C.2
Comparative statics in R

Now we turn to comparative statics in
R
.
For the case
R,S>1
it is useful to introduce an auxiliary function. For
q\in(0,\infty)\setminus\{1,2S/(S+1)\}
, define
c=c(q)
as the solution to the equation
O(q,c)/c=2/q
. Straightforward algebra leads to

\displaystyle c(q)=\ell(q)+\frac{(S-1)q}{2S-(S+1)q}(m(q)-\ell(q))=m(q)-\frac{2S(1-q)}{2S-(S+1)}(m(q)-\ell(q)).

(C.1)

The definition of
c
is extended to
q=0
and
q=1
via
c(0):=\lim_{q\downarrow 0}c(q)=\ell(0)=m(0)
and
c(1):=\lim_{q\to 1}c(q)=m(1)=\ell(1)
. Note that
c(q)
is continuous on
q\in[0,2S/(S+1))\supset(0,1)
and we have
c^{\prime}(0)=\ell^{\prime}(0)
and
c^{\prime}(1)=m^{\prime}(1)
. We also have

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ell(q)<c(q)<m(q),&q\in(0,1);\\
c(q)<m(q)<\ell(q),&q\in\left(1,\frac{2S}{S+1}\right);\\
m(q)<\ell(q)<c(q),&q\in\left(\frac{2S}{S+1},\infty\right).\end{cases}

Also, on
(0,1)\times(0,\infty)
,
\frac{O(q,z)}{z}>\frac{2}{q}
for
z\in(\ell(q),c(q))
and
\frac{O(q,z)}{z}<\frac{2}{q}
for
z\in(c(q),m(q))
. Further, on
(1,\frac{2S}{S+1})\times(0,m(q))
,
\frac{O(q,z)}{z}<\frac{2}{q}
on
(0,c(q)^{+})
and
\frac{O(q,z)}{z}>\frac{2}{q}
on
(c(q)^{+},m(q))
. Finally, on
(\frac{2S}{S+1},\infty)
,
\frac{O(q,z)}{z}<\frac{2}{q}
on
(0,m(q))
.

Lemma C.1
.

Suppose
\lambda>0
and the problem is well-posed such that
(n(\cdot),q_{*},q^{*})
is the solution to the free boundary problem in Proposition
5.3
. Then any of the following is a sufficient condition for
2n(q)-qn^{\prime}(q)>0
on
q\in[q_{*},q^{*}]
.

(a)

S<1
.

(b)

S>1
,
m(0)>0
, and either (recall the definition of
c(q)
in (
C.1
)):

(i)

2c(q)-qc^{\prime}(q)>0
on
(0,1)
if
q_{M}<1
or on
(0,\frac{2S}{S+1})
if
q_{M}>1
,

(ii)

R\geq 2
.

A further sufficient condition for (b)(i) is that
\ell^{\prime}(0)\geq 0
, or equivalently
(1-S)(\sigma-2\lambda)\geq 0
.

Proof.

(a)

The result under
R,S<1
is trivial since the solution
n(q)
is positive and decreasing (under
\lambda>0
).

(b)

We first show that
\ell^{\prime}(0)\geq 0
is sufficient for condition (b)(i) to hold. By direct calculation,

\displaystyle 2c(q)-qc^{\prime}(q)

\displaystyle=2\left[\frac{m(q)-\ell(q)}{\frac{2S}{S-1}\frac{1-q}{q}+1}+\ell(q)\right]-q\left[\frac{\left(\frac{2S}{S-1}\frac{1-q}{q}+1\right)(m^{\prime}(q)-\ell^{\prime}(q))+(m(q)-\ell(q))\frac{2S}{S-1}q^{-2}}{\left(\frac{2S}{S-1}\frac{1-q}{q}+1\right)^{2}}+\ell^{\prime}(q)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\frac{(S-1)q}{S\left(\frac{2S}{S-1}\frac{1-q}{q}+1\right)^{2}}+2\ell(q)-q\ell^{\prime}(q)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\frac{(S-1)q}{S\left(\frac{2S}{S-1}\frac{1-q}{q}+1\right)^{2}}+\frac{2(\delta+r(S-1))}{S}+\frac{S-1}{S}\left(\lambda\sigma-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\right)q

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\frac{(S-1)q}{S\left(\frac{2S}{S-1}\frac{1-q}{q}+1\right)^{2}}+2m(0)+\ell^{\prime}(0)q>0

on
q>0
away from
q=2S/(S+1)
.

(i)

Suppose
q_{M}\in(0,1)
such that
[q_{*},q^{*}]\subset(0,1)
. By the given condition,
O(q,c(q))=2c(q)/q>c^{\prime}(q)
for
q\in[q_{*},q^{*}]
. If
n^{+}_{(z)}=(n^{+}_{(z)}(q))_{q\geq z}
is the solution to the initial value problem
n^{\prime}=O(q,n)
with
n^{+}_{(z)}(z)=m(z)
,
n^{+}_{(z)}(q)
can then only upcross
c(q)
from below on
q\in(0,1)
. Since
c(0)=\ell(0)=m(0)
and the family of solutions
(n^{+}_{(z)}(\cdot))_{z}
is increasing in
z
, we must have
n(q)=n^{+}_{(q_{*})}(q)>n^{+}_{(0)}(q)\geq c(q)>\ell(q)
for all
q\in[q_{*},q^{*}]\subset(0,1)
. Finally,
\Xi(q,n):=\frac{O(q,n)}{n}=\frac{S-1}{S}\frac{1}{1-q}\frac{n-m(q)}{\ell(q)-n}
is continuously decreasing in
n\in(\ell(q),\infty)
for any fixed
q\in(0,1)
and therefore

\displaystyle\frac{n^{\prime}(q)}{n(q)}=\Xi(q,n(q))<\Xi(q,c(q))=\frac{2}{q}.

Suppose
q_{M}>1
and the transaction costs are sufficiently large such that
q_{*}<1<q_{M}<q^{*}
. Using the same argument as in the case of
q_{M}\in(0,1)
, we can still conclude
n(q)\geq c(q)
and in turn
n^{\prime}(q)/n(q)\leq 2/q
up to
q<1
. But since
n
now passes through the singular point
(1,m(1))
at which we have
c(1)=m(1)=n(1)
and
c^{\prime}(1)=m^{\prime}(1)=n^{\prime}(1)
, the ordering of
c(q)
and
n(q)
is ambiguous to the right of
q=1
. We thus need to first argue that
n(q)>c(q)
in the neighbourhood of
q=1
.

Write
f(q):=n(q)-c(q)
. Then

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(q)=n^{\prime}(q)-c^{\prime}(q)=\frac{S-1}{S}\frac{f(q)+c(q)}{1-q}\frac{f(q)+c(q)-m(q)}{\ell(q)-f(q)-c(q)}-c^{\prime}(q)

and a rearrangement of terms yields

\displaystyle\frac{f(q)}{(q-1)^{2}}=\frac{S(f^{\prime}(q)+c^{\prime}(q))}{(S-1)(f(q)+c(q))}\left(\frac{\ell(q)-c(q)}{1-q}-\frac{f(q)}{1-q}\right)-\frac{c(q)-m(q)}{(1-q)^{2}}.

(C.2)

Since it is known that
n(1)=m(1)=c(1)
and
n^{\prime}(1)=m^{\prime}(1)=c^{\prime}(1)
, we have
f(1)=f^{\prime}(1)=0
and
\lim_{q\to 1}f(q)/(1-q)=0
. Upon taking limit, we get

\displaystyle\lim_{q\to 1}\frac{f(q)}{(q-1)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{S(m^{\prime}(1)-\ell^{\prime}(1))m^{\prime}(1)}{(S-1)m(1)}-\frac{c^{\prime\prime}(1)-m^{\prime\prime}(1)}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{S(m^{\prime}(1)-\ell^{\prime}(1))m^{\prime}(1)}{(S-1)m(1)}-\frac{2S(m^{\prime}(1)-\ell^{\prime}(1))}{S-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{S(\ell^{\prime}(1)-m^{\prime}(1))}{S-1}\left[2-\frac{m^{\prime}(1)}{m(1)}\right]>0

because
\ell^{\prime}(1)>m^{\prime}(1)
and
2m(1)>m^{\prime}(1)
under the assumption of
m(0)>0
,
q_{M}>1
and
S>1
. Hence we conclude
n(q)>c(q)
near
q=1
.

Using similar arguments in the case of
q_{M}\in(0,1)
, we must have
\ell(q)>m(q)>n(q)>c(q)
on
1<q<q^{*}\wedge\frac{2S}{S+1}
. As
\Xi(q,n)=\frac{O(q,n)}{n}=\frac{S-1}{S}\frac{1}{1-q}\frac{n-m(q)}{\ell(q)-n}
is continuously decreasing in
n\in(0,\ell(q))
for any fixed
q>1
, we have
\frac{n^{\prime}(q)}{n(q)}=\frac{O(q,n(q))}{n(q)}<\frac{O(q,c(q))}{c(q)}=\frac{2}{q}
for
1<q<q^{*}\wedge\frac{2S}{S+1}
. If
q^{*}>\frac{2S}{S+1}
, then
c(q)>\ell(q)>m(q)>n(q)>0
on
q\in(\frac{2S}{S+1},q^{*})
. Note that
\Xi(q,n)
is discontinuous at
n=\ell(q)
. But since
H(q,n)
is still locally continuous and decreasing on
n\in[0,\ell(q))
and
n\in(\ell(q),\infty)
respectively under fixed
q>1
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{2}{q}=\Xi(q,c(q))>\lim_{n\to\infty}\Xi(q,n)=\frac{S-1}{S}\frac{1}{q-1}\geq\frac{S-1}{S}\frac{1}{q-1}\frac{m(q)}{\ell(q)}=\Xi(q,0)>\Xi(q,n(q))=\frac{n^{\prime}(q)}{n(q)}

on
\frac{2S}{S+1}<q<q^{*}
. By continuity of
n(q)
and
n^{\prime}(q)
, the above inequality holds at
q=\frac{2S}{S+1}
and
q=1
as well.

Finally, the case of
1\leq q_{*}<q_{M}<q^{*}
under small transaction costs can be handled similarly using the fact that the solution
n(q)
in such case is bounded below by
n^{+}_{(1)}(q)
.

(ii)

If
m(0)>0
and
\ell^{\prime}(0)\geq 0
then we are in Case b(i), so we only need to consider
m(0)>0
and
\ell^{\prime}(0)<0
. Since
\ell
is concave we have
m(1)=\ell(1)<\ell(0)=m(0)
so necessarily
q_{M}\in(0,1)
and
[q_{*},q^{*}]\subset(0,1)
. In what follows we only consider functions over
(0,1)
.

Define
A(q):=qm(q)+(1-q)\ell(q)
.
A
is chosen so that
A(z)=c(z)
and
A^{\prime}(z)=c^{\prime}(z)
for
z\in\{0,1\}
, and to be a simple function.
It is easily seen that
A(q)>c(q)
. So, if we can show that
n
is always above
A
it will follow that
2n(q)-qn^{\prime}(q)>0
on
(q_{*},q^{*})
as required.

Note that
O(q,A(q))=\frac{(S-1)}{S}\frac{A(q)}{q}
so that if
n=A
then
n^{\prime}>A^{\prime}
is equivalent to
\frac{(S-1)}{S}\frac{A(q)}{q}>A^{\prime}
. So, a sufficient condition for
n>A
on
(0,1)
is (here we use
m(q)-\ell(q)=Dq(1-q)
where
D=\frac{(S-1)}{S}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}
)

\displaystyle\frac{(S-1)}{S}\frac{A(q)}{q}>\ell^{\prime}(q)+(m(q)-\ell(q))+q(m^{\prime}(q)-\ell^{\prime}(q))

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle(S-1)(\ell(q)+q(m(q)-\ell(q)))>Sq\ell^{\prime}(q)+Sq(m(q)-\ell(q))+Sq^{2}(m^{\prime}(q)-\ell^{\prime}(q))

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle(S-1)\ell(q)>Sq\ell^{\prime}(q)+q(m(q)-\ell(q))+Sq^{2}(m^{\prime}(q)-\ell^{\prime}(q))

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle(S-1)\ell(0)+(S-1)q\ell^{\prime}(0)+(S-1)\frac{q^{2}}{2}\ell^{\prime\prime}(0)>Sq(\ell^{\prime}(0)+q\ell^{\prime\prime}(0))+Dq^{2}(1-q)+SDq^{2}(1-2q)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle(S-1)\ell(0)>q\ell^{\prime}(0)+\frac{q^{2}}{2}\ell^{\prime\prime}(0)(2S-(S-1))+Dq^{2}((1-q)+S(1-2q))

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle(S-1)\ell(0)>q\ell^{\prime}(0)+\frac{q^{2}}{2}\ell^{\prime\prime}(0)(S+1)+Dq^{2}((S+1)-q(1+2S)).

Thus we only need to establish
(S-1)\ell(0)>\sup_{q\in(0,1)}\varphi(q)
where
\varphi(q)
is a cubic function defined via

\varphi(q):=q\ell^{\prime}(0)+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2S}(S+1)(S-1)(2-R)q^{2}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2S}(S-1)(1+2S)q^{3},

where we use that
\frac{1}{2}\ell^{\prime\prime}(0)+D=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2S}(S-1)(2-R)
. Note that
\varphi(0)=0<(S-1)\ell(0)
and
\varphi^{\prime}(0)=\ell^{\prime}(0)<0
. Hence we are done if the coefficient of
q^{2}
is negative, which is equivalent to
R\geq 2
.

∎

Proof of Proposition
8.3
.

We will show that
\hat{p}_{*}\leq\tilde{p}_{*}
and
\hat{p}^{*}\leq\tilde{p}^{*}
under the stated conditions in Lemma
C.1
. The last part of the proposition will then follow trivially.

In what follows, we write

\displaystyle m(q)

\displaystyle=m(q;R)=\frac{\delta-r(1-S)}{S}-\frac{1-S}{S}\lambda\sigma q+R\frac{1-S}{S}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}q^{2}=m(q;0)+R\frac{1-S}{S}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}q^{2},

\displaystyle\ell(q)

\displaystyle=\ell(q;R)=\frac{\delta-r(1-S)}{S}-\frac{1-S}{S}\left(\lambda\sigma-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\right)q-(1-R)\frac{1-S}{S}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}q^{2}=\ell(q;0)+R\frac{1-S}{S}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}q^{2},

where
m(q;0)
and
\ell(q;0)
do not depend on
R
. Note that
\ell(q)-m(q)=\ell(q;0)-m(q;0)
.

Let
f(q):=\frac{n(q)-m(q)}{\ell(q)-n(q)}
where
n
is a solution to
n^{\prime}=O(q,n)
. Then

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(q)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\ell(q)-n(q))(n^{\prime}(q)-m^{\prime}(q))-(n(q)-m(q))(\ell^{\prime}(q)-n^{\prime}(q))}{(\ell(q)-n(q))^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+f(q)}{\ell(q)-m(q)}\left[\frac{S-1}{S(1-q)}f(q)(m(q)+\ell(q)f(q))-m^{\prime}(q)-\ell^{\prime}(q)f(q)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1+f(q)}{\ell(q;0)-m(q;0)}\Biggl[\frac{S-1}{S(1-q)}f(q)\left(m(q;0)+R\frac{1-S}{S}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}q^{2}+\left(\ell(q;0)+R\frac{1-S}{S}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}q^{2}\right)f(q)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt-m^{\prime}(q;0)-R\frac{1-S}{S}\sigma^{2}q-\left(\ell^{\prime}(q;0)+R\frac{1-S}{S}\sigma^{2}q\right)f(q)\Biggr]

\displaystyle=\Psi(q,f(q);0)-\frac{R(1+f(q))^{2}}{S(1-q)^{2}}\left((1-S)qf(q)+2S(1-q)\right)=:\Psi(q,f(q);R)

for some function
\Psi(q,f;R)
with
\Psi(q,f;0)
being independent of
R
. In particular,
\Psi
depends on
R
linearly and we have

\frac{\partial}{\partial R}\Psi(q,f;R)=-\frac{(1+f)^{2}}{S(1-q)^{2}}((1-S)qf+2S(1-q)).

Along a solution trajectory of the free boundary value problem,

\displaystyle(1-S)qf(q)+2S(1-q)

\displaystyle=(1-S)q\frac{n(q)-m(q)}{\ell(q)-n(q)}+2S(1-q)

\displaystyle=-Sq(1-q)\frac{O(q,n(q))}{n(q)}+2S(1-q)

\displaystyle=\frac{S(1-q)}{n(q)}\left(2n(q)-qn^{\prime}(q)\right).

Then if any condition in Lemma
C.1
holds such that
2n-qn^{\prime}>0
, we have
\sgn\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial R}\Psi(q,f(q);R)\right)=\sgn(q-1)
.

Recall the definitions of
(n^{+}_{(z)}(q))_{q\in[z,\zeta^{+}(q)]}
,
\zeta^{+}(z)
and
\Sigma^{+}(z)
defined in Proposition
A.1
or
A.2
. Set
f^{+}_{(z)}(q):=\frac{n^{+}_{(z)}(q)-m(q)}{\ell(q)-n^{+}_{(z)}(q)}
. Note that
\sgn(f^{+}_{(z)}(q))=\sgn(1-q)
. By construction,

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}q}f^{+}_{(z)}(q)=\Psi(q,f^{+}_{(z)}(q);R),\hskip 20.00003ptq\in[z,\zeta^{+}(z)],

f^{+}_{(z)}(z)=0=f^{+}_{(z)}(\zeta(z))
and

\Sigma^{+}(z)=\exp\left(\int_{z}^{\zeta^{+}(z)}\frac{f^{+}_{(z)}(q)}{q(1-q)}\,\mathrm{d}q\right).

Fix
\tilde{R}<\hat{R}
, and we will use the corresponding overhead notations to denote the particular quantities evaluated under
R=\tilde{R}
and
R=\hat{R}
respectively similar to what we have done in the proof of Proposition
8.1
.

If
\tilde{R}<\hat{R}
are such that
\tilde{q}_{M}\geq 1\geq\hat{q}_{M}
, then we obviously have
\hat{q}^{*}\leq 1\leq\tilde{q}^{*}
. It is therefore sufficient to only consider values of
\tilde{R},\hat{R}
such that
\tilde{q}_{M},\hat{q}_{M}<1
or
\tilde{q}_{M},\hat{q}_{M}>1
.

If
\tilde{q}_{M},\hat{q}_{M}<1
, then the domain of a solution to the free boundary problem must be contained within
(0,1)
. Since
\Psi(q,f^{+}_{(z)}(q);R)
is strictly decreasing in
R
on
q\in(0,1)
and
\hat{f}^{+}_{(z)}(z)=0=\tilde{f}^{+}_{(z)}(z)
with

\displaystyle(f^{+}_{(z)})^{\prime}(z)=-\frac{m^{\prime}(z;0)+R\frac{1-S}{S}\sigma^{2}z}{\frac{1-S}{S}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}z(1-z)}

(C.3)

which is decreasing in
R
such that
(\hat{f}^{+}_{(z)})^{\prime}(z)<(\tilde{f}^{+}_{(z)})^{\prime}(z)
for
z\in(0,1)
, we must have
\hat{\zeta}(z)\leq\tilde{\zeta}(z)<1
and
0\leq\hat{f}_{(z)}(q)\leq\tilde{f}^{+}_{(z)}(q)
for
q\in[z,\hat{\zeta}^{+}(z)]
. Then

\displaystyle\tilde{\Sigma}^{+}(z)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\int_{z}^{\tilde{\zeta}^{+}(z)}\frac{\tilde{f}^{+}_{(z)}(q)}{q(1-q)}\,\mathrm{d}q\right)\geq\exp\left(\int_{z}^{\hat{\zeta}^{+}(z)}\frac{\tilde{f}^{+}_{(z)}(q)}{q(1-q)}\,\mathrm{d}q\right)\geq\exp\left(\int_{z}^{\hat{\zeta}^{+}(z)}\frac{\hat{f}^{+}_{(z)}(q)}{q(1-q)}\,\mathrm{d}q\right)=\hat{\Sigma}^{+}(z)

and then
\hat{q}_{*}=(\hat{\Sigma}^{+})^{-1}(\xi)\leq(\tilde{\Sigma}^{+})^{-1}(\xi)=\tilde{q}_{*}
.

Suppose
\tilde{q}_{M},\hat{q}_{M}>1
. In case of
z>1
such that the domain of the solution lives on a subset of
(1,\infty)
,
\Psi(q,f^{+}_{(z)}(q);R)
is now increasing in
R
and
(\hat{f}^{+}_{(z)})^{\prime}(z)>(\tilde{f}^{+}_{(z)})^{\prime}(z)
for
z>1
using (
C.3
). Hence
\tilde{\zeta}(z)\geq\hat{\zeta}(z)>q_{M}
and
0\geq\hat{f}^{+}_{(z)}(q)\geq\tilde{f}^{+}_{(z)}(q)
for
1<z\leq q\leq\hat{\zeta}^{+}(z)
. Then we can deduce
\tilde{\Sigma}^{+}(z)\geq\hat{\Sigma}^{+}(z)
on
z>1
. The same conclusion can be extended to
z=1
upon showing that
(\hat{f}^{+}_{(1)})^{\prime}(1)>(\tilde{f}^{+}_{(1)})^{\prime}(1)
. It is sufficient to verify that

\lim_{q\to 1}\frac{f(q)}{q-1}=\frac{S}{1-S}\frac{m^{\prime}(1)}{m(1)}=\frac{R\sigma^{2}-\lambda\sigma}{R\frac{1-S}{S}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}+\frac{\delta-r(1-S)}{S}-\frac{1-S}{S}\lambda\sigma}

is increasing in
R
. One can check that the first order derivative of this expression with respect to
R
is proportional to

\displaystyle\frac{\delta-r(1-S)}{S}-\frac{\lambda\sigma(1-S)}{2S}=m(0)+\frac{m^{\prime}(0)}{2}=\ell(1)+\frac{1-S}{2S}R\sigma^{2}(q_{M}-1),

which is strictly positive in both cases of
S<1
(as
\ell(1)>0
under well-posedness and we work with
q_{M}>1
) and
S>1
(since
m^{\prime}(0)>0
, and
m(0)>0
as a a required condition).

Finally, for
\tilde{q}_{M},\hat{q}_{M}>1
and
z<1
such that
\tilde{\zeta}^{+}(z)=\tilde{\zeta}^{+}(1)\geq\hat{\zeta}^{+}(1)=\hat{\zeta}^{+}(z)
, we have
\tilde{f}^{+}_{(z)}(q)\geq\hat{f}^{+}_{(z)}(q)\geq 0
on
q\leq 1
,
\tilde{f}^{+}_{(z)}(1)=\hat{f}^{+}_{(z)}(1)=0
and
0\geq\hat{f}^{+}_{(z)}(q)=\hat{f}^{+}_{(1)}(q)\geq\tilde{f}^{+}_{(1)}(q)=\tilde{f}^{+}_{(z)}(q)
on
1\leq q\leq\hat{\zeta}(1)
. Then

\displaystyle\tilde{\Sigma}^{+}(z)=\exp\left(\int_{z}^{\tilde{\zeta}^{+}(z)}\frac{\tilde{f}^{+}_{(z)}(q)}{q(1-q)}\,\mathrm{d}q\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\int_{z}^{1}\frac{\tilde{f}^{+}_{(z)}(q)}{q(1-q)}\,\mathrm{d}q+\int_{1}^{\tilde{\zeta}^{+}(1)}\frac{\tilde{f}^{+}_{(z)}(q)}{q(1-q)}\,\mathrm{d}q\right)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(\int_{z}^{1}\frac{\hat{f}^{+}_{(z)}(q)}{q(1-q)}\,\mathrm{d}q+\int_{1}^{\hat{\zeta}^{+}(1)}\frac{\hat{f}_{(z)}(q)}{q(1-q)}\,\mathrm{d}q\right)=\hat{\Sigma}^{+}(z).

Hence
\tilde{\Sigma}^{+}(z)\geq\hat{\Sigma}^{+}(z)
for all
z
which establishes
\hat{q}_{*}\leq\tilde{q}_{*}
.

The result for the sale boundary can again be established by parametrising the solutions via their right boundary points.
∎
